Prediction-Powered Conditional Inference
Yang Sui, Jin Zhou, Hua Zhou, Xiaowu Dai

Abstract
We study prediction-powered conditional inference in the setting where labeled data are scarce, unlabeled covariates are abundant, and a black-box machine-learning predictor is available. The goal is to perform statistical inference on conditional functionals evaluated at a fixed test point, such as conditional means, without imposing a parametric model for the conditional relationship. Our approach combines localization with prediction-based variance reduction. First, we introduce a reproducing kernel-based localization method that learns a data-adaptive weight function from covariates and reformulates the target conditional moment at the test point as a weighted unconditional moment. Second, we incorporate machine-learning predictions through a correction-based decomposition of this localized moment, yielding a prediction-powered estimator and confidence interval that reduce variance when the predictor is informative while preserving validity regardless of predictor accuracy. We establish nonasymptotic error bounds and minimax-optimal convergence rates for the resulting estimator, prove pointwise asymptotic normality with consistent variance estimation, and provide an explicit variance decomposition that characterizes how machine-learning predictions and unlabeled covariates improve statistical efficiency. Numerical experiments on simulated and real datasets demonstrate valid conditional coverage and substantially sharper confidence intervals than alternative methods.

Key Words:
Conditional inference; Localization; Prediction-powered inference; Reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces; Uncertainty quantification.

1
Introduction

In many modern scientific and engineering applications, gold-standard labeled data are costly to obtain and therefore limited in number, whereas unlabeled covariates can often be collected at scale. At the same time, black-box machine-learning (ML) models can generate large numbers of inexpensive but imperfect predictions from covariates alone. This combination of scarce labels, abundant covariates, and readily available predictions arises in diverse domains, including genomics, medical imaging, proteomics, and materials discovery
(
Esteva et al. 2017
;
Jumper et al. 2021
;
Vaishnav et al. 2022
)
.
Motivated by this information structure, we study statistical inference for conditional functionals evaluated at a fixed covariate value. Let
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be labeled data drawn i.i.d. from the joint distribution
\rho
of
(X,Y)
on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, and let
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
be unlabeled covariates drawn i.i.d. from the marginal distribution
\rho_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In addition, suppose a black-box ML predictor
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is available, producing predictions
f(x)
for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

For a fixed covariate
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
, our goal is to use the three information sources
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\cup\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}\cup\{f\}
to conduct valid inference for a conditional functional
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
.
To illustrate, consider conditional mean inference, where the target is

\theta_{0}(x_{0}):=\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x_{0}].

A convenient characterization of
\theta_{0}(\cdot)
is through a conditional moment restriction. Let
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}
be a parameter space and let
\ell(Y;\theta)
be an estimating function. For the conditional mean, one may take
\ell(Y;\theta)=Y-\theta
, so that
\mathbb{E}[\ell(Y;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))|X=x_{0}]=0
. More generally, with an appropriate choice of
\ell
, this formulation covers a broad class of conditional functionals.
Unlike inference for global parameters defined through unconditional moments such as
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\ell(X,Y;\theta_{0})]=0
, conditional inference provides uncertainty quantification tailored to the fixed test point
x_{0}
. Conditional functionals are often the scientifically relevant objects in modern data-driven decision systems
(
Chakraborti
et al. 2025
)
. For example, in clinical risk assessment, a model that is well calibrated at the population level may still exhibit highly variable uncertainty across patients
(
Begoli et al. 2019
)
. Similarly, in economic and demographic analyses such as census income studies
(
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023a
)
, global summaries can obscure substantial variation across subpopulations. Conditional inference at specific covariate values provides uncertainty quantification aligned with this heterogeneity, rather than a single population-level summary.

\begin{overpic}[figs/PPCI_diagram.pdf]

\put(31.8,39.4){\footnotesize$Y$}
\put(37.8,39.4){\footnotesize$f(X)$}

\put(31.8,15.0){\footnotesize$\tilde{Y}$}
\put(37.8,15.0){\footnotesize$f(\tilde{X})$}
\put(47.8,15.0){\footnotesize$w_{x_{0}}(\tilde{X})$}
\par\put(90.0,37.5){\footnotesize$\theta_{0}(x_{0})$}
\put(88.5,29.0){\footnotesize$\mathcal{C}(x_{0})$}
\par\end{overpic}

Figure 1
:
Protocol for prediction-powered conditional inference. The procedure takes labeled data, unlabeled covariates, an ML predictor, and a test point
x_{0}
as inputs. A localization step uses the covariate distribution to learn weights that capture the local structure around
x_{0}
. The upper block estimates a bias correction from labeled data, while the lower block computes a plug-in term using predictions from the unlabeled data. These components are combined to produce a valid confidence interval
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
for the conditional target
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
.

In this paper, we develop a prediction-powered conditional inference (PPCI) framework that combines nonparametric localization with ML predictions. The approach converts the conditional moment defining the target at
x_{0}
into a localized unconditional moment using a weight function in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) learned from the covariate distribution. This enables pointwise inference without specifying a global model for the conditional functional and leverages abundant unlabeled covariates
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
to estimate the local structure around
x_{0}
.
Because localization reduces the effective sample size, naive localized estimators are typically variance-dominated. To address this, we incorporate ML predictions through a correction-based decomposition of the localized moment. The bias correction term, estimated from labeled data, depends on prediction residuals, while a plug-in term depending only on predictions is estimated from the large unlabeled sample. This decomposition reduces variance and yields a prediction-powered localized estimating equation whose solution
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
estimates
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
. The PPCI procedure is illustrated in Figure
1
.

We establish a nonasymptotic error bound for the PPCI estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
, treating it as the root of a localized estimating equation. The error decomposes into three components: a prediction-powered stochastic term governed by the labeled sample size
n
, a weight-learning error determined by the pooled sample size
n+N
, and an approximation bias controlled by the regularization parameter
\lambda
. The resulting convergence rate matches the minimax optimal rate for pointwise estimation.
We further prove pointwise asymptotic normality of the PPCI estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
with variance

\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{Var}\left(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\{\ell(Y;\theta)-\ell(f(X);\theta)\}\right)+\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{Var}\left(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\ell(f(X);\theta)\right),

which separates the contributions of labeled and unlabeled samples. In contrast, a labeled-only estimator has variance
(1/n)\mathrm{Var}(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\ell(Y;\theta))
. When
N\gg n
, the second term is negligible and the variance is driven by prediction residuals, yielding efficiency gains when the predictor
f
is informative.
We use these results to construct confidence intervals for
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
with asymptotically correct coverage. Under asymmetric data acquisition costs, we also derive a budget-optimal sampling strategy that minimizes interval width.

Our approach is closely related to prediction-powered inference (PPI), which provides general mechanisms for incorporating black-box ML predictions into statistical inference
(
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023a
;
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023b
;
Zrnic and Candès 2024
)
. Existing PPI methods primarily focus on global, population-level parameters and rely on full-sample averaging to improve statistical efficiency. Our work complements this line of research by extending the prediction-powered paradigm to pointwise conditional inference, where estimation is governed by the much smaller local effective sample size near
x_{0}
. To accommodate this local structure, we introduce a localization scheme that converts the conditional target into a weighted unconditional moment suitable for prediction-powered estimation. In this way, our framework adapts PPI principles to settings that require localized inference rather than global averaging.

We validate the proposed PPCI through extensive simulations and two real-world applications: Census income estimation and popularity prediction with the BlogFeedback dataset. We show that PPI exhibits undercoverage, particularly in the Census income task, where coverage falls well below nominal levels across age groups. Classical localized estimators based only on labeled data achieve nominal coverage but produce wide intervals due to limited effective local sample sizes. Our PPCI method improves this trade-off, producing substantially sharper intervals while preserving coverage across a range of conditional targets.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the background, including RKHS, conditional targets, and the prediction-powered framework.
Section
3
presents the proposed PPCI method. Section
4
establishes its estimation and inference results. Section
5
discusses practical considerations.
Section
6
compares our approach with related works.
Section
7
reports numerical experiments.
Section
8
concludes the paper with a discussion on future directions.

2
Background

In this section, we introduce the basic notation and RKHS background, define the conditional inference target of interest, and present the prediction-powered framework.

2.1
Notations

We use the following notations throughout the paper.
Let the input domain
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be convex, compact, and with nonempty interior. Let
\rho_{X}
be a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}
with density bounded above and below:

0<\rho_{0}\leq\frac{d\rho_{X}}{dx}(x)\leq\rho_{1}<\infty
.
Denote by
L^{2}(\rho_{X})
the Hilbert space of square-integrable functions on
\mathcal{X}
with inner product

\langle h,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}h(x)g(x)\,d\rho_{X}(x)
, and let
\|h\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|h(x)|
denote the supremum norm.
For vectors
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\|v\|_{2}
denotes the Euclidean norm. For matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
\mathrm{Tr}(A)
denotes the matrix trace. We write
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
and
a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}
.
For nonnegative sequences
\{a_{k}\}
and
\{b_{k}\}
, we write
a_{k}\lesssim b_{k}
or
a_{k}=O(b_{k})
if
a_{k}\leq c\,b_{k}
for some universal constant
c>0
, and
a_{k}\gtrsim b_{k}
if
a_{k}\geq c\,b_{k}
. We write
a_{k}\asymp b_{k}
if both hold,
a_{k}=O(1)
if
a_{k}
is bounded, and
a_{k}=o(b_{k})
if
a_{k}/b_{k}\to 0
.
For random sequences
\{A_{k}\}
and
\{B_{k}\}
with
B_{k}>0
almost surely, we write
A_{k}=O_{p}(B_{k})
if
\sup_{k}\mathbb{P}(|A_{k}/B_{k}|>M_{\varepsilon})<\varepsilon
for some finite
M_{\varepsilon}>0
and all
\varepsilon>0
, and
A_{k}=o_{p}(B_{k})
if
A_{k}/B_{k}\to 0
in probability.

2.2
Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS)

Let
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded, measurable, positive definite kernel, which satisfies

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}K(x,x)=\kappa^{2}<\infty
.
Let
\mathcal{H}
denote the associated separable RKHS, equipped with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(
Wahba 1990
)
.
For each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, define the canonical feature map
K_{x}:=K(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}
. The reproducing property holds:

h(x)=\langle h,K_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},\forall\,h\in\mathcal{H}
.
Define the integral operator
T_{K}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
by

(T_{K}h)(x)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}K(x,t)\,h(t)\,d\rho_{X}(t),h\in\mathcal{H}
.
A typical example of an RKHS is the Sobolev space
H^{m}(\mathcal{X})
of order
m>d/2
, which consists of functions

H^{m}(\mathcal{X})=\{h:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\ |\ h^{(j)}\text{ is continuous for }j=0,\ldots,m-1,\ \text{and}\ h^{(m)}\in L^{2}(\rho_{X})\}
.

2.3
Conditional Inference Target

Let
x_{0}
be an interior point of
\mathcal{X}
, and let
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}
be a parameter space. Our goal is to conduct inference for a conditional target
\theta_{0}(x_{0})\in\Theta
, defined as the unique solution to a conditional moment restriction at
x_{0}
. For clarity, we focus on scalar targets; extensions to vector-valued parameters follow by applying the same analysis componentwise
to each coordinate. Let
\ell:\mathcal{Y}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
be an estimating function and define the conditional moment as,

\eta(x_{0};\theta):=\mathbb{E}[\ell(Y;\theta)|X=x_{0}].

(1)

The target
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
is characterized as a root of
\eta(x_{0};\theta_{0}(x_{0}))=0
,
and our objective is to construct valid inference for
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
.
Many conditional functionals admit this representation. We consider the following examples:

•

Conditional mean:

Choosing
\ell(Y;\theta)=Y-\theta
yields
\theta_{0}(x_{0})=\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x_{0}]
.

•

Conditional log-odds in binary classification:

For
Y\in\{0,1\}
, taking
\ell(Y;\theta)=Y-(1+e^{-\theta})^{-1}
identifies
\theta_{0}(x_{0})=\log(\mathbb{P}(Y=1|X=x_{0})/\mathbb{P}(Y=0|X=x_{0}))
.

•

Conditional expected shortfall:

Let
Q_{\tau}(X)
denote the conditional
\tau
-quantile for
\tau\in(0,1)
and define

Y^{\ast}=Q_{\tau}(X)+\tau^{-1}(Y-Q_{\tau}(X))\mathbbm{1}\{Y\leq Q_{\tau}(X)\}
.
Choosing
\ell(Y^{\ast};\theta)=Y^{\ast}-\theta
yields

\theta_{0}(x_{0})=\mathbb{E}[Y^{\ast}|X=x_{0}]

(
He et al. 2023
;
Yu et al. 2025
)
.

Throughout, we assume that for each
\theta\in\Theta
, the function
x\mapsto\eta(x;\theta)
belongs to an RKHS
\mathcal{H}
.
For example, this assumption requires

x\mapsto\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x]\in\mathcal{H}
in the conditional mean setting,

x\mapsto\mathbb{P}(Y=1|X=x)\in\mathcal{H}
in binary classification,
and
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}[Y^{\ast}|X=x]\in\mathcal{H}
for conditional expected shortfall.

2.4
Prediction-Powered Framework

We consider a setting with three information sources: labeled data
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, unlabeled covariates
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
, and a black-box ML predictor
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. The datasets
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
are independent, and their covariates are drawn i.i.d. from the same marginal distribution
\rho_{X}
. The predictor
f
is treated as given. If
f
is trained using data overlapping with the labeled sample, standard sample-splitting techniques can be used to ensure that
f(X_{i})
is conditionally independent of
Y_{i}
given
X_{i}

(
Zrnic and Candès 2024
)
.

The prediction-powered framework leverages
f
to improve statistical efficiency. For a global parameter
\theta_{0}
defined by the moment condition
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\ell(X,Y;\theta_{0})]=0
, prediction-powered methods decompose the target moment as
(
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023a
)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\ell(X,Y;\theta)]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\ell(X,Y;\theta)-\ell(X,f(X);\theta)]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{X}}[\ell(X,f(X);\theta)].

The first term is estimated using labeled data, typically with reduced variance when
f
is informative. The second term is estimated using the large unlabeled sample, with variance diminishing as the unlabeled sample size grows.

In this paper, we extend the prediction-powered framework from global parameters to pointwise conditional inference for
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
. This extension raises two challenges. First, conditioning on
\{X=x_{0}\}
prevents expressing the conditional moment
\eta(x_{0};\theta)
in (
1
) as an unconditional average over labeled data
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Second, localization near
x_{0}
yields a small effective sample size and therefore high variance. To address these challenges, we introduce in the next section a localization scheme that converts (
1
) into an unconditional localized moment and combine it with prediction-powered estimation that leverages the predictor
f
and abundant unlabeled covariates to improve efficiency.

3
Prediction-Powered Conditional Inference

In this section, we detail our PPCI procedure for constructing valid confidence intervals for the conditional target
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
.

3.1
Algorithm

Our prediction-powered conditional inference (PPCI) procedure has three steps.
Step 1 constructs an RKHS-based localization weight that captures the covariate structure around test point
x_{0}
.
Step 2 converts the conditional moment defining the target into an unconditional localized moment, and applies a prediction-powered decomposition to incorporate the predictor
f
and the unlabeled covariates
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
for variance reduction.
Step 3 computes a confidence interval for the resulting conditional estimate.
We summarize our procedure in Algorithm
1
first, then discuss each step in detail.

Algorithm 1

Prediction-Powered Conditional Inference

\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, unlabeled covariates
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
,
predictor
f
, test point
x_{0}
, confidence level
(1-\alpha)
,
kernel
K(\cdot,\cdot)
, grid of regularization parameters
\Lambda
.

3:

4:

Randomly partition
\{1,\dots,N\}
into two equal-sized disjoint folds
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
.

6:

Compute
\Sigma_{t}^{(m)}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{I}_{m}|\times|\mathcal{I}_{m}|}
with
(\Sigma_{t}^{(m)})_{uu^{\prime}}=K(\tilde{X}_{u},\tilde{X}_{u^{\prime}})
, and
k_{0,t}^{(m)}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{I}_{m}|}
with
(k_{0,t}^{(m)})_{u}=K(\tilde{X}_{u},x_{0})
using only covariates in
\mathcal{I}_{m}
.

Select tuning parameter
\hat{\lambda}_{m}\in\Lambda
via the L-curve criterion on
\mathcal{I}_{m}
.

\hat{w}^{(m)}_{x_{0},\hat{\lambda}_{m}}(\cdot)=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{m}}K(\cdot,\tilde{X}_{u})(\xi_{\hat{\lambda}_{m}}^{(m)})_{u}
.

\overline{w}_{x_{0}}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{w}^{(1)}_{x_{0},\hat{\lambda}_{1}}(\cdot)+\hat{w}^{(2)}_{x_{0},\hat{\lambda}_{2}}(\cdot))
.

for

.

3.2
RKHS-Based Localization

We introduce an RKHS-based localization scheme that converts the conditional moment (
1
) at
x_{0}
into an unconditional weighted moment. Fix
\lambda>0
and define the localization weight

w_{x_{0},\lambda}:=(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K(x_{0},\cdot)\in\mathcal{H},

(2)

where
I
is the identity on
\mathcal{H}
and the inverse is defined via functional calculus
(
Smale and Zhou 2007
)
. Using
w_{x_{0},\lambda}
, we define the localized moment,

\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta):=\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,\ell(Y;\theta)\big],

(3)

which replaces conditioning on
\{X=x_{0}\}
with a tractable weighted expectation. This reformulation has a natural interpretation:
\eta_{\lambda}(\cdot;\theta)
exactly corresponds to the population Tikhonov-regularized approximation of the true conditional moment
\eta(\cdot;\theta)
in
\mathcal{H}
, given by

\eta_{\lambda}(\cdot;\theta)=\underset{h\in\mathcal{H}}{\arg\min}\Big\{\mathbb{E}[(h(X)-\eta(X;\theta))^{2}]+\lambda\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Big\}.

(4)

The regularization bias is therefore
\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)-\eta(x_{0};\theta)
. Hence, statistical inference for
\eta(x_{0};\theta)
can be based on
\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)
,
provided
\lambda
is chosen so that this bias is negligible.

In summary, the localization weight (
2
) converts the conditional moment (
1
) into the unconditional localized moment (
3
), which is the foundation for the prediction-powered conditional estimation developed next.

3.3
Prediction-Powered Conditional Estimation

We incorporate prediction-powered ideas into the localized moment (
3
) to improve efficiency in conditional estimation.
Using the predictor
f
, we decompose

\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\{\ell(Y;\theta)-\ell(f(X);\theta)\}\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\ell(f(X);\theta)\big].

(5)

To estimate the localization weights
w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)
, we employ two-fold cross-fitting on the unlabeled data; the extension to general multi-fold cross-fitting is straightforward.
We randomly split
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
into two disjoint folds
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
of equal size.
For each fold
m\in\{1,2\}
, we construct the empirical weight
\hat{w}^{(m)}_{x_{0},\lambda}
using only
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{m}}
.
For unlabeled observations in fold
\mathcal{I}_{m}
, we evaluate them using the out-of-fold weight
\hat{w}^{(3-m)}_{x_{0},\lambda}
trained on the other fold. For labeled data
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, the weights are constructed independently of the responses
Y_{i}
, and we evaluate labeled observations using the averaged weight

\overline{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{w}^{(1)}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\cdot)+\hat{w}^{(2)}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\cdot))
.
Replacing expectations in (
5
) by cross-fitted sample averages yields the empirical moment,

\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\big\{\ell(Y_{i};\theta)-\ell(f(X_{i});\theta)\big\}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{2}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{m}}\hat{w}^{(3-m)}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\ell(f(\tilde{X}_{u});\theta).

(6)

The prediction-powered conditional estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
is defined as the solution to

\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))=0.

(7)

3.4
Prediction-Powered Conditional Inference

Based on the prediction-powered estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
defined in (
7
), we construct a confidence interval for the conditional target
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
at the test point
x_{0}
.
We first compute the empirical Jacobian of the cross-fitted localized moment (
6
) evaluated at
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
:

\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0}):=\partial_{\theta}\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)\big|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}(x_{0})}.

(8)

Next, we define the empirical score contributions. For the labeled data,

\hat{\zeta}_{i}(x_{0}):=\overline{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\big\{\ell(Y_{i};\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))-\ell(f(X_{i});\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))\big\},\quad i=1,\dots,n,

and for the unlabeled data, if
u\in\mathcal{I}_{m}
with
m\in\{1,2\}
,

\hat{\zeta}_{u}(x_{0}):=\hat{w}^{(3-m)}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\,\ell(f(\tilde{X}_{u});\hat{\theta}(x_{0})).

Let
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{Y-f}(x_{0})
and
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{f}(x_{0})
denote the sample variances of

\{\hat{\zeta}_{i}(x_{0})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\hat{\zeta}_{u}(x_{0})\}_{u=1}^{N}
, respectively.
We estimate the variance of
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
by

\hat{V}(x_{0})=\frac{1}{n}\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{Y-f}(x_{0})+\frac{1}{N}\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{f}(x_{0}).

(9)

Finally, the
(1-\alpha)
PPCI confidence interval for
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
is

\mathcal{C}(x_{0}):=\Big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\,\sqrt{\hat{V}(x_{0})}/\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big|\Big),

(10)

where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
is the
(1-\alpha/2)
quantile of the standard normal distribution.

3.5
Parameter Tuning

We describe a data-driven procedure for selecting the regularization parameter
\lambda
in (
6
).
Because cross-fitting constructs separate weights for each fold of the unlabeled data, tuning is performed independently within each fold to obtain fold-specific parameters
\hat{\lambda}_{m}
.
Within a fold
\mathcal{I}_{m}
, the randomness arises from approximating the population operator
T_{K}
by its empirical counterpart
\hat{T}_{K}^{(m)}
based on
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{m}}
.
Standard cross-validation that treats
y_{u}=K(x_{0},\tilde{X}_{u})
as responses primarily measures approximation error and typically favors overly small
\lambda
, without accounting for the sampling variability of the downstream estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
.
Theoretical results in Section
4
show that the asymptotic variance of
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
scales with
(1/n+1/N)\mathbb{E}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)^{2}]
, so
\|\hat{w}^{(m)}_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}
acts as a variance proxy.
Accordingly,
\hat{\lambda}_{m}
should balance empirical approximation error and this variance proxy.

In practice, we use the L-curve method
(
Hansen 1992
)
.
For fold
\mathcal{I}_{1}
, let
\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\times|\mathcal{I}_{1}|}
and
k_{0,t}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{I}_{1}|}
denote the empirical kernel matrix and target vector constructed from
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}
, and define

\xi_{\lambda}^{(1)}=(\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}+|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\lambda I_{|\mathcal{I}_{1}|})^{-1}k_{0,t}^{(1)}
.
Over a grid of
\lambda
values, we plot two quantities:
the log empirical approximation error

\log\{\|\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}\xi_{\lambda}^{(1)}-k_{0,t}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}\}
,
and the log variance proxy

\log\{\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{1}|}\|\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}\xi_{\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}\}
,
which estimates
\log\|\hat{w}^{(1)}_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}
.
We select
\hat{\lambda}_{1}
at the point of maximum curvature and define

\hat{w}^{(1)}_{x_{0},\hat{\lambda}_{1}}(\cdot)=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}K(\cdot,\tilde{X}_{u})(\xi_{\hat{\lambda}_{1}}^{(1)})_{u}
.
The same procedure is applied to
\mathcal{I}_{2}
to obtain
\hat{w}^{(2)}_{x_{0},\hat{\lambda}_{2}}(\cdot)
. A detailed discussion on the computational cost for evaluating the L-curve criterion is provided in Appendix A.

4
Theoretical Guarantees

We provide theoretical guarantees for the proposed PPCI estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
and its associated inference procedure in this section.

4.1
Convergence Rates of Estimation

We show that the PPCI estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
defined in (
7
) achieves the minimax-optimal rate of convergence for pointwise estimation. We begin by introducing mild regularity conditions.

Assumption 1
.

There exists a compact neighborhood
\Theta_{0}\subset\Theta
containing
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
.
\forall\theta\in\Theta_{0}
, define
B_{Y-f}(\theta)=\sup_{(x,y)}|\ell(y;\theta)-\ell(f(x);\theta)|
and
B_{f}(\theta)=\sup_{x}|\ell(f(x);\theta)|
.
Then,
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}B_{Y-f}(\theta)<\infty
and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}B_{f}(\theta)<\infty
.

Assumption 2
.

On
\Theta_{0}
, the maps

\theta\mapsto\{\ell(y;\theta)-\ell(f(x);\theta)\}

and
\theta\mapsto\ell(f(x);\theta)

are continuously differentiable, and their derivatives are uniformly bounded. That is, by letting
G_{Y-f}(\theta)=\sup_{(x,y)}|\partial_{\theta}(\ell(y;\theta)-\ell(f(x);\theta))|
and
G_{f}(\theta)=\sup_{(x,y)}|\partial_{\theta}\ell(f(x);\theta)|
, we have
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}G_{Y-f}(\theta)<\infty
and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}G_{f}(\theta)<\infty
.

Assumption 3
.

Let
J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta):=\partial_{\theta}\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\mathbb{E}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,\partial_{\theta}\ell(Y;\theta)]
.
There exist constants
0<c_{J}\leq C_{J}<\infty
such that
c_{J}\leq|J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)|\leq C_{J}
for all
\theta\in\Theta_{0}
.

Assumption 4
.

Assume the conditional moment
\eta(\cdot;\theta)
belongs to RKHS
\mathcal{H}=H^{m}(\mathcal{X})
with smoothness
m>d/2
.
Its associated kernel
K
in (
2
) admits the eigendecomposition,

K(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mu_{j}\phi_{j}(x)\phi_{j}(x^{\prime})
, where the eigenfunctions
\{\phi_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
are orthonormal in
L^{2}(\rho_{X})
and bounded with
\sup_{j}\|\phi_{j}\|_{\infty}<\infty
, and the eigenvalues satisfy
\mu_{j}\asymp j^{-2m/d}
.

Assumption 5
.

There exists a constant
\underline{\sigma}^{2}>0
such that

\mathrm{Var}\big(\ell(Y;\theta)|X\big)\geq\underline{\sigma}^{2}
almost surely for all
\theta\in\Theta_{0}
.

Assumption
1
ensures uniform boundedness of the loss
\ell
, while Assumption
2
guarantees smoothness and boundedness of its derivative with respect to
\theta

(
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023b
)
. Assumption
3
guarantees local identifiability by requiring the localized Jacobian to be bounded away from zero near
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
. Assumption
4
specifies the functional space with its associated eigenvalue decay rate and requires uniformly bounded eigenfunctions, which are standard regularity conditions in RKHS analysis
(
Wahba 1990
;
Wainwright 2019
)
. Finally, Assumption
5
ensures that the conditional variance remains strictly non-degenerate. We rigorously verify that all these regularity conditions hold for our three running examples in Section
2.3
in Appendix C.

A key quantity in our analysis is the
pointwise leverage
at the test point
x_{0}
,

D(x_{0};\lambda):=\big\langle K_{x_{0}},(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

In classical RKHS regression problems, complexity is typically measured by the
global effective dimension

D(\lambda):=\mathrm{Tr}((T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}T_{K})

(
Caponnetto and
De Vito 2007
;
Wainwright 2019
;
Fischer and Steinwart 2020
)
, which characterizes the estimation of the entire regression function on
\mathcal{X}
. In contrast, our objective is a pointwise estimate at
x_{0}
. A direct identity yields
D(x_{0};\lambda)=\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}+\lambda\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
, demonstrating that
D(x_{0};\lambda)
strictly bounds both the
L^{2}(\rho_{X})
variance proxy and the RKHS smoothness penalty of the localization weight. Furthermore, the eigenvalues of
(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}
scale as
(\mu_{j}+\lambda)^{-1}
, which strictly increase as
\lambda\to 0
. Intuitively, as the regularization level
\lambda
vanishes, the weight function
w_{x_{0},\lambda}
sharpens to approximate a Dirac delta function at
x_{0}
, leading its
L^{2}(\rho_{X})
-norm to diverge. Consequently,
D(x_{0};\lambda)
grows and the pointwise estimation problem becomes highly variable, whereas a larger
\lambda
reduces this leverage and stabilizes the estimation.

Proposition 1
.

Let
x_{0}
be an interior point of
\mathcal{X}
. As
\lambda\to 0
,

D(x_{0};\lambda)\asymp D(\lambda)\asymp\lambda^{-d/(2m)}
.

Proposition
1
shows that for RKHS
\mathcal{H}=H^{m}(\mathcal{X})
and interior
x_{0}
, the pointwise leverage
D(x_{0};\lambda)
has the same asymptotic order as the global effective dimension
D(\lambda)
.

Minimax Upper Bound of Estimation.

We first establish a nonasymptotic upper bound for the PPCI estimator

\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
in (
7
). The bound separates moment estimation error,
weight estimation error, and regularization bias, and shows how labeled data,
unlabeled data, and the predictor
f
jointly determine the estimation rate.

Theorem 1
.

Let
x_{0}
be an interior point of
\mathcal{X}
, and let
\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})
denote the locally unique solution in
\Theta_{0}
to
\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=0
. Suppose Assumptions
1
–
4
hold. Assume the regularization parameter satisfies

\lambda^{-1}=o\left(\left(\frac{N}{\log N}\right)^{\frac{m}{d}}\wedge\left(\frac{n\wedge N}{\log N}\right)^{\frac{2m}{d}}\right).

Then for all sufficiently large
n,N
, with probability at least
1-53/N-4/n^{2}-8/N^{2}
,
we have

\displaystyle|\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})|\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{c_{J}}\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log(n+N)}\left(\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{N}}\right)}_{\text{moment estimation error}}

(11)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{32\sqrt{2}}{c_{J}}\kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N}{N}}}_{\text{weight estimation error}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{c_{J}}\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\lambda D(x_{0};\lambda)}}_{\text{regularization bias}}.

Moreover, under the optimal choice of
\lambda\asymp\left(\mathscr{N}/\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{2}
, where

\mathscr{N}:=\sqrt{\log(n+N)}\left(\frac{8\sqrt{2}B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{8\sqrt{2}B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{N}}\right)+\frac{32\sqrt{2}\kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\log N}}{\sqrt{N}},

collects the stochastic terms from labeled and unlabeled data,
there exists
C_{0}>0
such that

|\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{C_{0}}}{c_{J}}\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{d}{2m}}\mathscr{N}^{1-\frac{d}{2m}}.

(12)

We make three remarks on Theorem
1
. First, the bound in (
11
) has a clear statistical interpretation.
The first term of (
11
) represents the error in estimating the localized moment (
6
), which depends on the residual size
B_{Y-f}
and the prediction magnitude
B_{f}
. When the unlabeled sample size
N
is large and the predictor
f
is informative, the residual term
B_{Y-f}
is small, resulting in substantial error reduction.
The second term of (
11
) is the error from estimating the population localization weight (
2
) by its empirical version.
The third term of (
11
) is the regularization bias induced by Tikhonov regularization in (
3
).

Second, in contrast to classical kernel ridge regression (KRR) that focuses on estimation of the regression function itself
(
Wahba 1990
;
Dai and Li 2023
;
Ma et al. 2023
;
Dai 2025
)
, we target a moment-defined functional
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
in (
1
) at a fixed design point
x_{0}
. This difference induces two additional difficulties.
On one hand, the statistical property of estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
depends on the localized Jacobian

J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\partial_{\theta}\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)
in Assumption
3
.
Because
J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)
involves derivatives of the loss, it generally lies outside the RKHS
\mathcal{H}
. As a result, standard uniform convergence results for KRR do not directly control its empirical approximation. To ensure that the empirical Jacobian consistently approximates its population counterpart, we establish the scaling condition

\lambda^{-1}=o\left((N/\log N)^{m/d}\right)
in Theorem
1
.
On the other hand, the estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
couples the estimated localization weights
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
with the loss
\ell(f(\cdot);\theta)
evaluated on the same data in the empirical moment (
6
), inducing a nontrivial dependence structure. We address this via cross-fitting: weights estimated on one fold are applied exclusively to the other. This decoupling ensures that the conditionally weighted moments behave as independent sums, allowing separate control of the moment estimation error and the weight estimation error in Theorem
1
.

Finally, we analyze a PPCI procedure without sample-splitting in Appendix D, in which all covariates from both labeled and unlabeled samples are pooled to construct the localization weights
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
. Although this approach is fully data-efficient, it significantly complicates the theoretical analysis.
The main challenge is the shared-design dependence between the labeled and unlabeled components. The empirical localization weight
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
is learned from the pooled covariates, and is therefore not independent of either the labeled score terms
\{\ell(Y_{i};\theta)-\ell(f(X_{i});\theta)\}_{i=1}^{n}
or the unlabeled prediction-score terms
\{\ell(f(\tilde{X}_{u});\theta)\}_{u=1}^{N}
in the empirical moment (
6
). Consequently, the empirical moment involves dependent terms, and controlling them requires more delicate arguments. In Appendix E.3, we summarize the technical novelties of our theoretical analysis and provide a detailed comparison with classical KRR theory.

Minimax Lower Bound of Estimation.

We now establish a minimax lower bound, showing that the PPCI estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
in (
7
) is rate-optimal over a class of regular distributions.
Let
\mathcal{P}
denote the class of distributions
(\rho_{X},\rho_{Y|X},K)
satisfying Assumptions
4
and
5
, together with the constraint

\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq B_{\eta}
for some constant
B_{\eta}\in(0,\infty)
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
v_{n,N}:=1/n+1/(n+N)
.
There exists a constant
c>0
, depending only on
(\kappa,B_{\eta},\underline{\sigma}^{2})
, such that for all
n\geq 1
and
N\geq 0
,

\inf_{\tilde{\theta}}\ \sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[(\tilde{\theta}-\theta_{0}(x_{0}))^{2}\big]\geq c\inf_{\lambda>0}\Big\{v_{n,N}\cdot\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)^{2}]\vee B_{\eta}^{2}\,\lambda\,\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}D_{P}(x_{0};\lambda)\Big\},

(13)

where the infimum is taken over all measurable estimators based on
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
.
Under
\mathcal{H}=H^{m}(\mathcal{X})
, the right-hand side scales as

\inf_{\lambda>0}\big\{v_{n,N}\lambda^{-d/(2m)}\vee B_{\eta}^{2}\lambda^{1-d/(2m)}\big\}\asymp v_{n,N}^{\,1-d/(2m)}
, up to constants depending on
(m,d,B_{\eta})
.

We make two remarks on Theorem
2
. First, this lower bound reveals a bias-variance tradeoff. The variance term

v_{n,N}\cdot\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)^{2}]
captures the intrinsic estimation error of the target
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
. The bias term
B_{\eta}^{2}\,\lambda\cdot\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}D_{P}(x_{0};\lambda)
reflects the bias introduced by regularization in RKHS. Balancing these two terms yields the canonical minimax rate

\inf_{\tilde{\theta}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}[(\tilde{\theta}-\theta_{0}(x_{0}))^{2}]\gtrsim\big(1/n+1/(n+N)\big)^{1-d/(2m)}

(
Wahba 1990
)
. This lower bound remains valid when
N=0
.

Second, we compare the upper bound in Theorem
1
with the minimax lower bound in Theorem
2
. Up to logarithmic terms, the stochastic factor satisfies
\mathscr{N}\asymp n^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}
in Theorem
1
. Substituting this into (
12
) yields two distinct regimes:

•

Regime 1 (
n\asymp N
or
N/n\to\infty
):
Here,
\mathscr{N}\asymp n^{-1/2}
, yielding a mean squared error of
O(n^{-1+d/(2m)})
, which matches the lower bound in Theorem
2
since
v_{n,N}\asymp n^{-1}
.

•

Regime 2 (
n/N\to\infty
):
The unlabeled data becomes the bottleneck (
\mathscr{N}\asymp N^{-1/2}
), yielding a mean squared error of
O(N^{-1+\frac{d}{2m}})
, which matches the lower bound in Theorem
2
since
v_{n,N}\asymp N^{-1}
.

Thus, Theorem
1
is minimax optimal up to logarithmic factors.

4.2
Coverage Guarantees of Confidence Interval

We now establish asymptotic normality of the PPCI estimator

\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
and the validity of the resulting confidence interval.

Theorem 3
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
1

and Assumptions
1
–
5
,
let

J_{\lambda}(x_{0})=J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})),\quad V(x_{0}):=\frac{1}{n}\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+\frac{1}{N}\sigma^{2}_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})),

where
\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}(\theta)=\mathrm{Var}\big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\{\ell(Y;\theta)-\ell(f(X);\theta)\}\big)
and
\sigma^{2}_{f}(\theta)=\mathrm{Var}\big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,\ell(f(X);\theta)\big)
.
Suppose

n=o\left(\frac{N}{\log N}\right),\quad\lambda=o(n^{-1}),\quad\lambda^{-1}=o\left(\left(\frac{N}{\log N}\right)^{\frac{m}{d}}\wedge\left(\frac{n}{(\log(n+N))^{2}}\right)^{\frac{2m}{d}}\right).

(14)

Then, as
n,N\to\infty
,

\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})\big)}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}\to N(0,1).

We make three remarks on Theorem
3
. First, compared to Theorem
1
, the asymptotic normality in Theorem
3
characterizes the limiting distribution rather than providing a worst-case upper bound. In Theorem
1
, we explicitly bound the moment estimation error in (
11
). Taking the unlabeled data as an example, the variance of the empirical moment is
\sigma^{2}_{f}(\theta)/N=\mathrm{Var}\big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\ell(f(X);\theta)\big)/N
. To construct the first term in (
11
), we use Assumption
1
to decouple the loss function from the localization weights, yielding the relaxation
\mathrm{Var}\big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\ell(f(X);\theta)\big)/N\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\{w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\ell(f(X);\theta)\}^{2}\big]/N
that is further bounded by
B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)^{2}\big]/N\lesssim B_{f}^{2}(\theta)D(x_{0};\lambda)/N
. Consequently, the bounding constants
B_{Y-f}
and
B_{f}
explicitly appear in the first term of (
11
). In contrast, Theorem
3
establishes a central limit theorem where the exact variance components
\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}
and
\sigma^{2}_{f}
directly govern the limiting behavior. While Assumption
1
is still inherited to ensure finite moments, the conservative bounds
B_{Y-f}
and
B_{f}
are replaced by the precise limiting variance
V(x_{0})
. Furthermore, Theorem
3
imposes Assumption
5
to guarantee that
V(x_{0})
is non-degenerate.

Second, the condition
n=o(N/\log N)
ensures that the error from estimating the localization weights is asymptotically negligible. The condition
\lambda=o(n^{-1})
imposes undersmoothing so that the regularization bias is dominated by the variance. To clarify the scaling, suppose
N=n^{r_{1}}
and
\lambda=n^{r_{2}}
with
r_{1}>0
,
r_{2}<0
. These conditions are satisfied if

r_{1}>1,\quad-\frac{m}{d}\min\{r_{1},2\}<r_{2}<-1.

The requirement
r_{1}>1
is natural in the abundant-unlabeled regime
N\gg n
. Moreover, when
r_{1}\geq 2
, the interval
-\frac{m}{d}\min\{r_{1},2\}<r_{2}<-1
is always nonempty since
2m>d
.

Finally, in practice, we replace
J_{\lambda}(x_{0})
and
V(x_{0})
by their estimates
\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})
in (
8
) and

\hat{V}(x_{0})
in (
9
), both evaluated at
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
,
yielding the PPCI confidence interval
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
in (
10
).

Corollary 1
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
3
,

\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})\to_{p}J_{\lambda}(x_{0})
and

\hat{V}(x_{0})\to_{p}V(x_{0})
.
Moreover, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\mathbb{P}\big(\theta_{0}(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\big)\to 1-\alpha
,
where
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
is the PPCI confidence interval constructed in (
10
).

This corollary validates the asymptotic coverage of the PPCI confidence interval.

5
Practical Implications

In this section, we clarify how the ML predictor and the unlabeled data contribute to PPCI, and discuss how to sample labeled and unlabeled data under a limited budget.

5.1
The Role of ML Predictor

The asymptotic variance
V(x_{0})
in Theorem
3

characterizes how the ML predictor
f
affects estimation uncertainty.
For the classical localized estimator based only on labeled data, the variance is
n^{-1}\sigma_{Y}^{2}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
, with
\sigma_{Y}^{2}(\theta)=\mathrm{Var}\big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\ell(Y;\theta)\big)

(
Tsybakov 2009
)
. In contrast, the PPCI variance decomposes as

V(x_{0})=\frac{1}{n}\sigma_{Y-f}^{2}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+\frac{1}{N}\sigma_{f}^{2}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
,
where
\sigma_{Y-f}^{2}(\theta)=\mathrm{Var}\big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\{\ell(Y;\theta)-\ell(f(X);\theta)\}\big)
and
\sigma_{f}^{2}(\theta)=\mathrm{Var}\big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\ell(f(X);\theta)\big)
. When
N\gg n
, the second term
\sigma_{f}^{2}/N
is negligible, and the variance
is dominated by the residual component
\sigma_{Y-f}^{2}
.
If the predictor
f
is informative in the sense that
\sigma_{Y-f}^{2}(\theta)\ll\sigma_{Y}^{2}(\theta)
, then PPCI enjoys substantial variance reduction relative to the classical localized estimator.
Thus, efficiency gains arise precisely when the predictor meaningfully reduces
residual variability.

Figure 2
:
Empirical
\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}
,
\sigma^{2}_{Y}
, and
\sigma^{2}_{f}
for the conditional mean
at different (age, sex) test points in the census income data.
Results are based on
1000
replications.

We illustrate this phenomenon using the census income data in Section
7.2
. Using XGBoost as the auxiliary predictor
f
, we estimate
\sigma_{Y}^{2}
,
\sigma_{Y-f}^{2}
, and
\sigma_{f}^{2}
. Figure
2
shows that
\sigma_{Y-f}^{2}
is substantially smaller than
\sigma_{Y}^{2}
, especially for the male subgroup (
\text{sex}=1
, with
\text{sex}=2
representing the female subgroup). In this regime,
\sigma_{f}^{2}/N
is negligible, and PPCI achieves clear variance reduction.

The gain depends on the quality of the predictor
f
. If
f
is uninformative, the residual variance
\sigma_{Y-f}^{2}(\theta)
can be large. To account for this, one may introduce a tuning parameter
\omega\in[0,1]
and modify the empirical moment in (
6
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\,\ell(Y_{i};\theta)-\omega\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\,\ell(f(X_{i});\theta)\\
&\quad+\omega\,\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\,\ell(f(\tilde{X}_{u});\theta).\end{split}

When
\omega=1
, it coincides with the PPCI estimator;
when
\omega=0
, it reduces to the classical localized estimator.

5.2
The Role of Unlabeled Data

The unlabeled data
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
are incorporated to estimate the localization weights
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
. Recall from (
2
) that the population weight
w_{x_{0},\lambda}
relies on the population operator
T_{K}
. In Step 1 of Algorithm
1
, we approximate
T_{K}
using the empirical operator
\hat{T}_{K}
constructed from these unlabeled samples. As established in Theorem
1
, the resulting weight estimation contributes an
O_{p}(\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)/N})
term to the total error. Thus, a large unlabeled sample enables a more accurate estimation of the underlying localization structure.
Moreover, unlabeled data directly reduce estimation variance and improve efficiency of PPCI estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
, whose variance is

V(x_{0})=\frac{1}{n}\sigma_{Y-f}^{2}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+\frac{1}{N}\sigma_{f}^{2}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
,
the term
\sigma_{f}^{2}(\theta)/N
vanishes as
N
increases.

Figure 3
:
Empirical coverage rate and average width of nominal
95\%
confidence intervals for the conditional mean at the test point (
\text{age}=70,\text{sex}=1
) in the census income data. The red horizontal line indicates the nominal
95\%
coverage level. Results are based on
1000
replications.

We illustrate these effects by revisiting the census income data in Section
7.2
. Fixing a test point corresponding to age
70
and sex
1
, we increase the unlabeled sample size
N
from
500
to
10,500
.
Figure
3
shows a clear pattern: as
N
increases, the average confidence interval width decreases, while coverage remains stable. This result highlights the practical benefit of abundant unlabeled data in improving efficiency.

5.3
Budget-Aware Sampling Design

We study how to allocate labeled and unlabeled samples under a fixed budget to minimize the expected width of the PPCI confidence interval in (
10
). Let
c_{l}>0
and
c_{u}>0
denote the per-sample costs of labeled and unlabeled data, and let
C>0
be the total budget. Formally, the empirical budget-optimal allocation problem aims to solve

\min_{n>0,N>0}\sqrt{\hat{V}(x_{0})}/|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})|

subject to
c_{l}n+c_{u}N\leq C
.
While this empirical objective depends on the finite-sample estimates
\hat{V}(x_{0})
and
\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})
, Corollary
1
shows that
{\sqrt{\hat{V}(x_{0})}}/{|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})|}={\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}/{|J_{\lambda}(x_{0})|}(1+o_{p}(1))
. We focus on minimizing the deterministic leading-order term
\sqrt{V(x_{0})}/|J_{\lambda}(x_{0})|
. Because the population Jacobian
J_{\lambda}(x_{0})
depends solely on
(x_{0},\lambda)
, minimizing the leading-order width is equivalent to minimizing the variance
V(x_{0})
under the budget constraint.

Proposition 2
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
3
and write
\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}=\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
and
\sigma^{2}_{f}=\sigma^{2}_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
, the budget-optimal allocation for
\min_{n>0,N>0}\sqrt{V(x_{0})}/|J_{\lambda}(x_{0})|

subject to
c_{l}n+c_{u}N\leq C

is

n^{\star}=\frac{C\,\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}/c_{l}}}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}\,c_{l}}+\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{f}\,c_{u}}}
and

N^{\star}=\frac{C\,\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{f}/c_{u}}}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}\,c_{l}}+\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{f}\,c_{u}}}
,
with minimum variance
V_{\min}(C)=\frac{\big(\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}\,c_{l}}+\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{f}\,c_{u}}\big)^{2}}{C}.

In Proposition
2
, the population variances
\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}
and
\sigma^{2}_{f}
depend on
(x_{0},\lambda)
and are unknown in practice.
To implement the optimal allocation, we adopt a two-stage design. First, we use a small fraction of the total budget to collect preliminary labeled and unlabeled samples and compute the empirical variances
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{Y-f}
and
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{f}
of the localized score contributions.
We then plug these estimates into Proposition
2
to determine the budget-optimal allocation
(n^{\star},N^{\star})
under the remaining budget. Finally, we collect a new dataset of sizes
(n^{\star},N^{\star})
and apply the full PPCI procedure in Algorithm
1
.

6
Comparisons with Alternative Methods

In this section, we compare the proposed PPCI with three related works: classical PPI, conformal prediction, and synthetic-surrogate methods. We highlight the challenges of conditional inference and clarify the specific advantages of PPCI.

6.1
Prediction-Powered Inference

Classical PPI
(
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023a
)
targets a global parameter
\theta_{0}
defined by the moment condition
\mathbb{E}[\ell(X,Y;\theta_{0})]=0
. A natural extension to conditional settings is to estimate the conditional moment
\eta(x;\theta)=\mathbb{E}[\ell(Y;\theta)|X=x]
in (
1
) using both labeled and unlabeled covariates through a regression method such as KRR, and then evaluate the estimate at a test point
x_{0}
.

From an augmented inverse-propensity-weighted (AIPW) perspective

(
Robins and
Rotnitzky 1995
)
, suppose we observe
N^{\prime}=n+N
covariates

\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}
but only
n
labels
\{Y_{i}:O_{i}=1\}
, where

O_{i}\in\{0,1\}
is independent of
(X_{i},Y_{i})
with
\mathbb{P}(O_{i}=1)=\pi=n/N^{\prime}
.
Define the pseudo-outcome

Z(\theta)=\ell(f(X);\theta)+\frac{O}{\pi}\{\ell(Y;\theta)-\ell(f(X);\theta)\}.

Then
\mathbb{E}[Z(\theta)|X=x]=\eta(x;\theta)
in (
1
).
As shown in Appendix E.1,

\mathrm{Var}(Z(\theta)|X=x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\mathrm{Var}(\ell(Y;\theta)|X=x)+\frac{1-\pi}{\pi}\big(\eta(x;\theta)-\ell(f(x);\theta)\big)^{2}.

(15)

To study the implications for conditional inference, consider the
KRR estimator. Let
\bar{X}=\{\bar{X}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}
denote the combined covariates, where
\bar{X}_{j}=X_{j}
for
j\leq n
and

\bar{X}_{j}=\tilde{X}_{j-n}
for
j>n
. The estimator can be written as

\hat{\eta}(x_{0};\theta)=k_{0}^{\top}(\Sigma+\gamma N^{\prime}I_{N^{\prime}})^{-1}\bar{Z}(\theta)=\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\xi_{j}(x_{0})\bar{Z}_{j}(\theta)
,
where
\Sigma=K(\bar{X},\bar{X})
is the kernel matrix,

k_{0}=K(\bar{X},x_{0})
, and

\xi(x_{0})=(\Sigma+\gamma N^{\prime}I)^{-1}k_{0}
are the smoothing weights.
Conditional on
\bar{X}
, the pseudo-outcomes are independent, so

\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\eta}(x_{0};\theta)|\bar{X})=\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\xi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}\mathrm{Var}(\bar{Z}_{j}(\theta)|\bar{X}_{j})
.
Combining this with (
15
) yields

\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\eta}(x_{0};\theta)|\bar{X})\geq\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\xi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}\mathrm{Var}(\ell(Y;\theta)|X=\bar{X}_{j}).

This bound explains why the naive extension of PPI to conditional inference
fails to achieve substantial variance reduction. Let

N^{\prime}_{\mathrm{eff}}(x_{0}):=(\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\xi_{j}(x_{0})^{2})^{-1}
denote the local
effective sample size of the smoother. If the conditional variance varies
smoothly near
x_{0}
, then

\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\eta}(x_{0};\theta)|\bar{X})\gtrsim\frac{1}{\pi\,N^{\prime}_{\mathrm{eff}}(x_{0})}\mathrm{Var}(\ell(Y;\theta)|X=x_{0})\approx\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{eff}}(x_{0})}\mathrm{Var}(\ell(Y;\theta)|X=x_{0}),

where
n_{\mathrm{eff}}(x_{0})\propto n/D(x_{0};\lambda)
.
Thus the variance is effectively governed by the labeled sample size,
eliminating the variance reduction from the unlabeled data.

In contrast, the PPCI estimator in Theorem
3

achieves variance approximately

\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{eff}}(x_{0})}\mathrm{Var}(\ell(Y;\theta)-\ell(f(X);\theta))+\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{eff}}(x_{0})}\mathrm{Var}(\ell(f(X);\theta)),

where
N_{\mathrm{eff}}(x_{0})\propto N/D(x_{0};\lambda)
.
Since
N\gg n
, the dominant term becomes the residual variance

\mathrm{Var}(\ell(Y;\theta)-\ell(f(X);\theta))
, which can be much smaller than

\mathrm{Var}(\ell(Y;\theta))
when the predictor
f
is informative.
Consequently, PPCI effectively leverages unlabeled data to achieve
substantial variance reduction for conditional inference at
x_{0}
.

6.2
Conformal Prediction

Conformal prediction provides distribution-free guarantees for predicting
Y
given
x
by constructing prediction sets with marginal coverage
(
Vovk et al. 2005
;
Lei et al. 2018
)
, and more recently, conditional coverage guarantees
(
Gibbs et al. 2025
)
. However, its focus is on predictive coverage for the realized outcome
Y
. Our objective is different: we aim to conduct inference for conditional functionals. For instance, in the conditional mean setting, the target is
\theta_{0}(x_{0})=\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x_{0}]
, which may differ substantially from the observed outcome
Y
when the noise in response is large.

Figure 4
:
Diabetes progression data illustration. Left: split conformal
predictive intervals for the outcome
Y
at test covariates
X
. Right: a bootstrap uncertainty band for the conditional mean

\theta_{0}(x)=\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x]
.

This distinction is practically important. In clinical decision support, practitioners need more than a range of plausible responses
Y
; they require a reliable, patient-specific risk estimate
\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x_{0}]
together with its uncertainty
(
Begoli et al. 2019
)
.
To illustrate, consider the diabetes progression dataset with
n=442
patients and
d=10
baseline covariates
(
Efron et al. 2004
)
, where
Y
measures disease progression one year after baseline. Let
\theta_{0}(x)=\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x]
denote the conditional mean. Figure
4
compares (left) a split conformal prediction interval for
Y

(
Lei et al. 2018
)
and (right) a bootstrap uncertainty band for
\theta_{0}(x)
, both evaluated at the same test points. The predictive intervals remain wide across covariates, reflecting substantial response variability, whereas the uncertainty band for
\theta_{0}(x)
is considerably tighter. Consequently, response-level coverage can be overly conservative for decision-making. For example, two patients may have clearly different estimated mean progression, yet their predictive intervals for
Y
may be wide and heavily overlapping. In such cases, predictive coverage gives an ambiguous prognosis, while conditional inference for
\theta_{0}(x)
provides uncertainty quantification that is directly aligned with action.

6.3
Synthetic-Surrogate Methods

We also compare PPCI with methods that use surrogate or synthetic outcomes to address partially missing responses, such as SynSurr
(
McCaw et al. 2024
)
. SynSurr studies genetic association when the true phenotype
Y
is observed only for a subset of individuals, while a synthetic surrogate
\hat{Y}
is available for all. Let
Z=(G,X)
denote genotype and covariates. SynSurr assumes a joint linear-Gaussian model

\begin{bmatrix}Y\\
\hat{Y}\end{bmatrix}\Big|Z=\begin{bmatrix}Z^{\top}\beta\\
Z^{\top}\alpha\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\varepsilon_{T}\\
\varepsilon_{S}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\begin{bmatrix}\varepsilon_{T}\\
\varepsilon_{S}\end{bmatrix}\sim N(0,\Sigma),

where
\Sigma
captures the correlation between the target noise
\varepsilon_{T}
and surrogate noise
\varepsilon_{S}
.
The efficiency gain hinges on
\hat{Y}
containing residual variation beyond
Z
. Writing
\hat{Y}=Z^{\top}\alpha+\varepsilon_{S}
, SynSurr leverages the residual
\varepsilon_{S}
and its correlation with
\varepsilon_{T}
to improve inference for
\beta
. If instead
\hat{Y}
is deterministic given
Z
, so that
\varepsilon_{S}=0
, then
\mathrm{Var}(\varepsilon_{S})=0
and the residual-coupling mechanism collapses.
This distinction is particularly relevant in modern ML pipelines, where
\hat{Y}
is produced by a pretrained ML predictor that maps
Z
to
\hat{Y}
without an explicit stochastic residual component. In such settings, SynSurr may offer limited improvement over standard regression on
Z
.
Furthermore, implementing the joint model requires specifying the relationship between
\hat{Y}
and
Z
and estimating the residual covariance structure. When
\hat{Y}
is generated by a black-box ML predictor, restricting this relationship to a linear or other parametric class can introduce misspecification and potentially degrade finite-sample performance.

In contrast, PPCI performs conditional inference at
x_{0}
without imposing a joint parametric model for
(Y,\hat{Y})
or requiring an explicit stochastic model for the surrogate. It avoids modeling the surrogate mechanism and directly targets the conditional functional of interest.

7
Experiments

We evaluate PPCI through both simulation studies and real-data applications to assess its performance for conditional inference.

7.1
Simulation Studies

The covariates are generated as
X=(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})^{\top}\sim\mathrm{Unif}([0,1]^{3})
and the response is
Y=\eta(X)+\varepsilon
, where

\displaystyle\eta(X)=\sin(2\pi X_{1})+0.6\cos(2\pi X_{2})+0.4\sin(2\pi X_{3})+0.25\sin(2\pi(X_{1}+X_{2})),

and
\varepsilon\sim N(0,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})
with
\sigma_{\varepsilon}=2.0
is independent of
X
.
The auxiliary predictor is constructed as
f(X)=Y+b(X)+\xi
, where
\xi\sim N(0,\sigma_{f}^{2})
with
\sigma_{f}=1.2
, independent of
(X,Y)
, and the bias term is
b(X)=0.2\{(X_{1}-0.5)+0.5\sin(4\pi X_{2})-0.3(X_{3}-0.5)\}
.

For each test point
x_{0}
, we use
n=200
labeled data and
N=10000
unlabeled data. The PPCI estimator is implemented as in Algorithm
1
, with a Matérn
5/2
kernel,

K_{h}(x,z)=(1+\sqrt{5}{\|x-z\|_{2}}/{h}+\frac{5}{3}{\|x-z\|_{2}^{2}}/{h^{2}})\exp(-\sqrt{5}{\|x-z\|_{2}}/{h})
. For each
x_{0}
, a pilot labeled sample of size
200
is used to select
h
via leave-one-out cross-validation (LOOCV). Given
\hat{h}
, the regularization parameter
\lambda
is chosen using the L-curve criterion over a logarithmic grid of order
O(n^{-1})
.
To obtain
1000
replications per test point, we generate the unlabeled covariates independently five times. For each unlabeled sample, localization weights are computed once, and then
200
independent labeled samples are drawn. In each draw, auxiliary predictions are regenerated for both labeled and unlabeled observations.

Figure 5
:
Empirical RMSE, coverage rate, and average width of nominal 95% confidence intervals for the conditional mean evaluated at different test points in the simulated data. Results are based on 1000 replications per test point.

We compare PPCI with two benchmarks. The first is a labeled-only (LO) conditional estimator. For a fair comparison, LO uses the same averaged localization weights
\overline{w}_{x_{0}}
constructed by Algorithm
1
, but discards all unlabeled predictions. The second benchmark is the classical PPI method
(
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023a
)
, which uses the labeled data with all
N=10000
unlabeled predictions, but targets a marginal functional and ignores localization at
x_{0}
.
We evaluate performance on test points
x_{0}\in[0.70,0.85]^{3}
. Selecting 10 equally spaced values per coordinate yields 1000 test points. Figure
5
reports the empirical RMSE, coverage rate, and average width of nominal 95% confidence intervals across these points. PPCI consistently produces narrower intervals than LO, demonstrating substantial variance reduction from leveraging predictions and unlabeled data. In contrast, global PPI shows undercoverage under conditional evaluation.

7.2
Census Income Data

We study conditional mean inference for income as a function of age (
1
–
99
) and sex (Male
=1
, Female
=2
) using the California census data from
(
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023a
)
. The dataset contains
380091
observations with XGBoost predictions
(
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023b
)
. The outcome
Y
is rescaled by dividing by
10000
. The sample sizes are
187471
(sex
=1
) and
192620
(sex
=2
), with mean incomes
5.30
and
3.35
, respectively; a two-sample
t
-test yields a
p
-value of order
10^{-6}
, indicating a substantial income gap. All analyses are conducted separately within each sex group.
When many observations share the same (age, sex), the sample mean can approximate the conditional mean. However, this is infeasible at sparse or unobserved points. To assess coverage, we construct data-driven oracle targets by fitting a Nadaraya–Watson smoother with a Matérn-
5/2
kernel on the full dataset. After standardization, we define

\theta_{0}(x_{0})=\big[\sum_{i}K_{\bar{h}}(X_{i},x_{0})\,Y_{i}\big]/\big[\sum_{i}K_{\bar{h}}(X_{i},x_{0})\big]
,
where
\bar{h}=\mathrm{median}\{\|X_{i}-x_{0}\|_{2}\}
. These oracle targets are used solely for evaluation and are not available in practice.

Figure 6
:
Empirical RMSE, coverage rate, and average width of nominal
95\%
confidence
intervals for the conditional mean
at different (age, sex) test points in the census income data.
Results are based on
1000
replications.

We focus on ages
70
–
100
, where data are sparser and inference is more challenging. For each test point
x_{0}=(\text{age},\text{sex})
, all exact duplicates are excluded from the sampling pool. We then draw
n=300
labeled and
N=10000
unlabeled data from the remaining data, using the precomputed predictions
f(X)
from
(
Angelopoulos
et al. 2023b
)
. PPCI is implemented via Algorithm
1
. A pilot sample of
300
labeled data per sex group is used to select the bandwidth
h
by LOOCV, and the replication scheme matches that in Section
7.1
.

We compare PPCI with the labeled-only (LO) conditional estimator and the classical PPI method. Here, LO uses the same averaged localization weights
\overline{w}_{x_{0}}
but excludes unlabeled predictions. Figure
6
reports RMSE, coverage, and average interval width over ages
70
–
100
. Global PPI produces intervals that are largely insensitive to
x_{0}
, demonstrating its marginal target. In contrast, both PPCI and LO adapt to age. Coverage is close to nominal for both conditional methods, while PPCI consistently achieves smaller RMSE and shorter confidence intervals across test points.

7.3
BlogFeedback Data

We apply PPCI to conditional mean inference in a high-dimensional text regression problem using the BlogFeedback dataset from the UCI repository
(
Buza 2013
)
. Each observation corresponds to a blog post, with response equal to the number of comments received within 24 hours. We use the log-transformed outcome
Y=\log(1+\text{comments})
to reduce skewness. The covariate vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{280}
contains engineered temporal, activity-based, and content-related features, all standardized to zero mean and unit variance. A LightGBM regressor
(
Ke et al. 2017
)
is trained on the full dataset, and its predictions are treated as the auxiliary predictor
f(x)=\hat{Y}
. After preprocessing, the dataset contains
14{,}526
posts with covariates
X
, outcomes
Y
, and predictions
\hat{Y}
.

The conditional mean
\theta_{0}(x_{0})=\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x_{0}]
is unknown, and most covariate profiles appear only once, making direct plug-in estimates highly variable. As in Section
7.2
, we construct a data-driven oracle target using a Nadaraya–Watson smoother fitted on the full dataset for evaluation purposes. We select 50 test points. For each
x_{0}
, all exact duplicates are excluded from the sampling pool. We then draw
n=300
labeled and
N=10{,}000
unlabeled observations from the remaining data, using their associated predictions. PPCI is implemented via the two-fold cross-fitting procedure in Algorithm
1
. A fixed pilot sample of 300 labeled observations is used to select the bandwidth
h
by LOOCV, and the replication scheme matches that in Section
7.1
.

Figure 7
:
Empirical RMSE, coverage rates, and average widths of nominal
95\%

confidence intervals for the conditional mean at test points in the
BlogFeedback data. Results are based on
1000
replications for each test
point.

Figure
7
reports RMSE, coverage, and average interval width across the 50 test points. As in Section
7.2
, the classical PPI method produces intervals that are nearly insensitive to
x_{0}
. In contrast, both PPCI and the labeled-only (LO) estimator adapt to local structure. Compared with LO, PPCI attains comparable coverage while yielding substantially shorter intervals and smaller RMSE, highlighting the benefit of leveraging unlabeled data and informative predictions.

8
Conclusion

In this paper, we propose a prediction-powered framework for conditional inference that integrates labeled data, unlabeled covariates, and ML predictions. The procedure is explicitly tailored to a fixed test point
x_{0}
, with localization and uncertainty quantification designed at the pointwise level. We establish theoretical guarantees, including minimax-optimal error bounds of estimation and asymptotic normality, and show empirically that the resulting confidence intervals achieve near-nominal coverage while delivering substantial efficiency gains by leveraging ML predictors and unlabeled covariates.

We discuss a few directions for further investigation. First, the current construction does not adapt to the quality of the predictor. Developing principled, data-driven methods for weighting the contribution of the ML predictor is of interest. Second, our theory relies on smooth estimating functions. Extending the framework to nonsmooth targets, such as conditional quantiles, without resorting to smoothing approximations is also of interest.

Data Availability Statement

The BlogFeedback data are available in the UCI Machine Learning Repository at
https://doi.org/10.24432/C58S3F
. The Census income data are available in the Prediction-Powered Inference (
ppi_py
) repository at
https://github.com/aangelopoulos/ppi_py
, and can be accessed with the dataset identifier
census_income
. The simulated data and code are available at
https://github.com/YS-stat/PPCI
.

The appendices are organized as follows:

•

Appendix
A
details the implementation of the L-curve criterion discussed in Section
3.5
. We provide both a geometric illustration and an analysis of the computational cost.

•

Appendix
B
presents additional numerical experiments.

•

Appendix
C
provides the verification of the assumptions introduced in Section
4
.

•

Appendix
D
details the full procedure of the PPCI framework without sample-splitting, establishing its methodology, theoretical guarantees, and complete proofs.

•

Appendix
E
provides the detailed proofs for all theoretical results in the main text, and also summarizes the technical novelties of our theoretical framework.

Appendix A
Implementation Details of the L-curve Criterion

This section provides details on the L-curve criterion used for tuning the regularization parameter
\lambda
. We first illustrate the intuition of the method using a simulated toy example in Appendix
A.1
. Subsequently, Appendix
A.2
details an efficient spectral decomposition strategy designed to minimize the computational cost of the grid search.

A.1
Illustration of the L-curve Criterion

To illustrate the above L-curve criterion, we consider a toy example and set

|\mathcal{I}_{1}|=2000
and the covariate dimension to
10
.
The covariates
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}
and the target point
x_{0}

are generated independently from the standard normal distribution.
The kernel
K
is chosen as the Gaussian kernel with bandwidth
0.3
.
For each
\lambda
on a logarithmic grid, we compute the two L-curve coordinates
in the log–log plane: the log residual norm and the log variance proxy.
As shown in Figure
8
, the curve exhibits the characteristic
L-shape, and the L-corner represents the selected regularization level.

Figure 8
:

L-curve criterion for selection of the regularization parameter
\lambda
. (i) the L-curve in the log residual norm–log
L^{2}
norm plane; (ii) the geometric distance to the straight line joining the
endpoints of the L-curve, whose maximizer defines the selected
\lambda
.

A.2
Computational Cost and Efficient Implementation

Taking the first fold (
m=1
) as example, tuning the regularization parameter
\lambda
via the L-curve criterion requires evaluating two norms: the approximation error
\|\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}\xi_{\lambda}^{(1)}-k_{0,t}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}
and the variance proxy
\|\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}\xi_{\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}
. A naive implementation solving the linear system for each
\lambda
incurs a prohibitive cost of
O(|\Lambda|\cdot|\mathcal{I}_{1}|^{3})
.

To achieve scalability, we exploit the spectral decomposition
\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}=UDU^{\top}
, where
U
is orthogonal (
U^{\top}U=I
) and
D=\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},\dots,d_{|\mathcal{I}_{1}|})
. We first precompute the projected target vector
\tilde{k}=U^{\top}k_{0,t}^{(1)}
. The coefficient vector is analytically given by
\xi_{\lambda}^{(1)}=U(D+|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\lambda I)^{-1}\tilde{k}
.
Because the orthogonal matrix
U
preserves
L^{2}
norms (
\|Ux\|_{2}^{2}=\|x\|_{2}^{2}
), we can evaluate both L-curve criteria in the spectral domain. This completely bypasses the matrix-vector multiplications required to reconstruct
\xi_{\lambda}^{(1)}
. Specifically, the variance proxy and approximation error simplify to scalar operations:

\displaystyle\|\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}\xi_{\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|D(D+|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\lambda I)^{-1}\tilde{k}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{|\mathcal{I}_{1}|}\left(\frac{d_{j}}{d_{j}+|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\lambda}\tilde{k}_{j}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle\|\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}\xi_{\lambda}^{(1)}-k_{0,t}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|D(D+|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\lambda I)^{-1}\tilde{k}-\tilde{k}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{|\mathcal{I}_{1}|}\left(\frac{-|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\lambda}{d_{j}+|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\lambda}\tilde{k}_{j}\right)^{2}.

Constructing
\Sigma_{t}^{(1)}
takes
O(|\mathcal{I}_{1}|^{2})
time. The single eigendecomposition dominates with
O(|\mathcal{I}_{1}|^{3})
time. Precomputing
\tilde{k}
takes
O(|\mathcal{I}_{1}|^{2})
. Crucially, because evaluating each candidate
\lambda
only uses the scalar formulas above, scanning the entire grid
\Lambda
takes merely
O(|\Lambda|\cdot|\mathcal{I}_{1}|)
operations. Thus, our implementation effectively decouples the grid search from matrix multiplications, reducing the total time complexity per fold to
O(|\mathcal{I}_{1}|^{3})
, strictly dominated by the one-time eigendecomposition. The memory complexity is bounded by
O(|\mathcal{I}_{1}|^{2})
.

Appendix B
Supplementary Experiments: Conditional Quantile Inference

Our proposed PPCI framework can also be extended to conditional quantile settings.
In conditional quantile inference, the estimating score

\ell_{\tau}(Y;\theta)=\tau-\mathbbm{1}\{Y\leq\theta\},\qquad\tau\in(0,1)

is non-smooth.
To place conditional quantiles within our framework, we instead work with a smoothed quantile score.
A simple choice, used in our experiments, is logistic smoothing.
Let
S(z)=1/(1+e^{-z})
denote the logistic CDF and let
h>0
be a bandwidth.
Define
\ell_{\tau,h}(Y;\theta):=\tau-S((\theta-Y)/h)
.
For any fixed
h>0
, the map
\theta\mapsto\ell_{\tau,h}(Y;\theta)
is continuously differentiable with a uniformly bounded derivative, so Assumptions
2
and
3
hold on
\Theta_{0}
.

More generally, one may replace
S(z)
with any smooth, bounded kernel, which again yields a smoothed score satisfying our assumptions.
Relaxing these smoothness assumptions to directly handle non-smooth scores such as
\ell_{\tau}
is an interesting direction for future work.
In the following, we conduct simulation studies and a census income data application to assess conditional quantile inference.

B.1
Simulation Studies

As in Section
7.1
, we adopt the same data generation process to evaluate confidence intervals for the conditional median, i.e.,
\theta_{0}(x_{0})=Q_{Y|X=x_{0}}(\tau)
with
\tau=0.5
. Under this design, the true parameter explicitly evaluates to
\theta_{0}(x_{0})=\eta(x_{0})+\sigma_{\varepsilon}\,\Phi^{-1}(\tau)
, where
\Phi(\cdot)
denotes the standard normal cumulative distribution function (CDF). To handle the non-differentiability of the standard quantile loss, the indicator function in the estimating equation is replaced by a smooth logistic-CDF approximation.

Because solving the smoothed non-linear estimating equations for conditional quantiles is computationally demanding, we adopt a simple strategy for this experiment. Specifically, for each test point
x_{0}
, we allocate a labeled sample of size
n=200
and an unlabeled budget of size
N=7000
. The unlabeled data is partitioned into two disjoint sets:
\mathcal{I}_{1}
of size
5000
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
of size
2000
. The larger set
\mathcal{I}_{1}
is utilized to construct the localization weight function
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
and tune the regularization parameter
\hat{\lambda}
, while
\mathcal{I}_{2}
is reserved for evaluating the PPCI estimator alongside the labeled data. To ensure computational feasibility across replications, the weight-learning set
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and the selected tuning parameters
(\hat{h},\hat{\lambda})
are drawn once and held fixed for each test point
x_{0}
. In contrast, the labeled sample and the evaluation set
\mathcal{I}_{2}
are independently redrawn in each replication. Empirical performance is evaluated over 500 replications per test point. All other implementation details remain strictly identical to the conditional mean inference setup described in Section
7.1
.

Figure
9
summarizes the empirical RMSEs, coverage rates, and average widths of nominal
95\%
confidence intervals for the conditional median across all test points.
Overall, PPCI achieves coverage close to the nominal level while attaining smaller RMSEs and narrower intervals than LO.
In contrast, LO can exhibit comparable coverage in some regions but typically incurs larger RMSEs and wider intervals.

Figure 9
:
Empirical RMSE, coverage rate, and average width of nominal 95% confidence intervals for the conditional median evaluated at different test points in the simulated data. Results are based on 500 replications per test point.

B.2
Census Income Data

Unlike the synthetic setting, the true conditional targets

\theta_{0}(x_{0})=Q_{Y|X=x_{0}}(\tau)
are unknown at any
test point
x_{0}
.
Following Section
7.2
, we fit
a Nadaraya–Watson (NW) smoother using a Matérn kernel with smoothness parameter
5/2
on the full
dataset for the conditional
\tau
-quantile.
Throughout the experiment, these oracle values are treated as

\theta_{0}(x_{0})
when computing empirical coverage.

For conditional quantile inference, the focus is on the conditional median
(
\tau=0.5
).
The oracle targets
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
are
constructed in the same spirit.
The same logistic smoothing scheme as in
Appendix
B.1
is used for the indicator in the quantile score. Following Appendix
B.1
, we adopt a streamlined strategy. At each test point, we draw a labeled sample of size
n=200
and an unlabeled sample of size
N=10{,}000
. The unlabeled sample is partitioned into two disjoint sets:
\mathcal{I}_{1}
of size
7000
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
of size
3000
. The larger set
\mathcal{I}_{1}
is utilized to construct the localization weight function
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
and tune the regularization parameter
\hat{\lambda}
, while the remaining set
\mathcal{I}_{2}
is reserved for evaluating the PPCI estimator alongside the labeled data. All other implementation details and tuning parameters for PPCI and the LO baseline remain strictly identical to the conditional mean inference setup described in Section
7.2
.

Figure
10
reports the empirical RMSEs, coverage rates, and average interval widths for the conditional median across
ages
70
–
100
.
The global PPI baseline again performs poorly.
The LO estimator tends to be conservative for conditional quantile estimation and produces
wider intervals.
PPCI maintains coverage close to the nominal level while achieving smaller RMSEs and yielding
substantially shorter and more stable intervals, reflecting the benefit of
leveraging the predictions and unlabeled data.

Figure 10
:
Empirical RMSE, coverage rate, and average width of nominal
95\%
confidence
intervals for the conditional median
at different (age, sex) test points in the census income data.
Results are based on
500
replications.

Appendix C
Verification of Assumptions in Section
4

Assumptions
1
and
2
:
First, consider the residual term
\ell(y;\theta)-\ell(f(x);\theta)
. In each example,
\theta
enters the loss as an additive shift. For the conditional mean and binary log-odds, the difference is
y-f(x)
. For conditional expected shortfall, it becomes
y^{\ast}-f^{\ast}(x)
, where
f^{\ast}(x)
predicts
Y^{\ast}
. In all cases,
\theta
cancels out, so
G_{Y-f}(\theta)\equiv 0
, and
B_{Y-f}(\theta)<\infty
provided responses and predictions are bounded.
Second, for the predictor term
\ell(f(x);\theta)
, the
\partial_{\theta}\ell(f(x);\theta)
equals either
-1
or
-p(\theta)(1-p(\theta))
, where
p(\theta)=(1+e^{-\theta})^{-1}
. Since
\Theta_{0}
is compact, these derivatives are uniformly bounded. If the predictions are bounded, then
B_{f}(\theta)
is also finite. Hence both assumptions hold.

Assumption
3
:
For the conditional mean and expected shortfall,
\partial_{\theta}\ell(\cdot;\theta)=-1
, so
|J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)|=\mathbb{E}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)]=1_{\lambda}(x_{0})
, where
1_{\lambda}
is the Tikhonov-regularized approximation of the constant function
1
in (
4
). Since
1\in H^{m}(\mathcal{X})
for a compact
\mathcal{X}
, standard regularization theory for integral operators
(
Caponnetto and
De Vito 2007
)
implies that
1_{\lambda}(x_{0})
is strictly bounded away from zero and infinity for sufficiently small
\lambda>0
. For the binary log-odds,
\partial_{\theta}\ell(\cdot;\theta)=-p(\theta)\{1-p(\theta)\}
with
p(\theta)=(1+e^{-\theta})^{-1}
, yielding
|J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)|=p(\theta)\{1-p(\theta)\}\,1_{\lambda}(x_{0})
. If
\mathbb{P}(Y=1|X=x_{0})
is bounded away from
0
and
1
, then
p(\theta)\{1-p(\theta)\}
is bounded away from zero in a neighborhood of
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
, verifying the assumption.

Assumption
5
:
For conditional mean, it reduces to
\mathrm{Var}(Y|X)\geq\underline{\sigma}^{2}
. For binary log-odds, it becomes
\mathbb{P}(Y=1|X)\mathbb{P}(Y=0|X)\geq\underline{\sigma}^{2}
.
For conditional expected shortfall, it translates to
\mathrm{Var}(Y^{\ast}|X)\geq\underline{\sigma}^{2}
, meaning that the lower-tail distribution of
Y
below its
\tau
-quantile remains non-degenerate.

Appendix D
PPCI Without Sample-Splitting

As established in Section
3
, the cross-fitted PPCI estimator proposed in Section
4
achieves the desired asymptotic normality and variance reduction. Nevertheless, because the cross-fitting strategy partitions the unlabeled covariates into disjoint folds, the fold-specific empirical weights do not fully exploit the entire pool of available covariates. To address this, we further investigate a variant of the PPCI procedure without sample-splitting in this appendix. Specifically, we construct a global empirical weight function,
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
, by pooling all available covariates from both the labeled and unlabeled datasets. The remainder of this appendix is organized as follows: Appendix
D.1
introduces this full-sample methodology. Appendix
D.2
provides the detailed theoretical guarantees for this full-sample pointwise estimator. Appendix
D.3
contains the formal proofs of the theory presented in Appendix
D.2
, and Appendix
D.4
provides the proofs of the technical lemmas utilized in Appendix
D.3
.

D.1
Methodology

To construct the empirical localization weight corresponding to (
2
) in Section
3.2
,
let
N^{\prime}:=n+N
and define the pooled covariate sample by

\bar{X}_{j}=X_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,n
and
\bar{X}_{j}=\tilde{X}_{j-n}
for
j=n+1,\dots,N^{\prime}
.
The empirical integral operator of kernel
K
is
