\hat{T}_{K}:=\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}K_{\bar{X}_{j}}\otimes K_{\bar{X}_{j}}
,
and the empirical localization weight is

\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}:=(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K(x_{0},\cdot)
.
Equivalently, let
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}\times N^{\prime}}
be the pooled Gram matrix

\Sigma_{jk}=K(\bar{X}_{j},\bar{X}_{k})
and let
k_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}
satisfy

(k_{0})_{j}=K(\bar{X}_{j},x_{0})
. Then we have the empirical weights for (
2
) as

\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\cdot)=K(\cdot,\bar{X})(\Sigma+\lambda N^{\prime}I_{N^{\prime}})^{-1}k_{0}.

The empirical localized moment is obtained by replacing the expectations in (
5
) with sample averages and using
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
. Specifically,

\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\big\{\ell(Y_{i};\theta)-\ell(f(X_{i});\theta)\big\}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\ell\big(f(\tilde{X}_{u});\theta\big).

(16)

The prediction-powered conditional estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
is defined as the solution to

\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))=0.

(17)

Based on the prediction-powered estimator
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
defined as the solution to (
17
), we construct conditional inference for
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
at test point
x_{0}
.
First, we compute the empirical Jacobian of the localized moment (
16
) evaluated at
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
:

\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0}):=\partial_{\theta}\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)\big|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}(x_{0})}.

(18)

Second, define the empirical contributions from the labeled and unlabeled samples to (
16
) as

\hat{\zeta}_{i}(x_{0}):=\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\big\{\ell(Y_{i};\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))-\ell(f(X_{i});\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))\big\}
with
i=1,\dots,n
,
and
\hat{\zeta}_{u}(x_{0}):=\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\,\ell(f(\tilde{X}_{u});\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))
, with
u=1,\dots,N
.
Let
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{Y-f}(x_{0})
and
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{f}(x_{0})
denote the sample variances of

\{\hat{\zeta}_{i}(x_{0})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\hat{\zeta}_{u}(x_{0})\}_{u=1}^{N}
, respectively.
The variance estimator is then

\hat{V}(x_{0}):=\frac{1}{n}\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{Y-f}(x_{0})+\frac{1}{N}\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{f}(x_{0}).

(19)

Finally, the
(1-\alpha)
conditional confidence interval for
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
is constructed as

\mathcal{C}(x_{0}):=\Big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\,\sqrt{\hat{V}(x_{0})}/\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big|\Big),

(20)

where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
denotes the
(1-\alpha/2)
quantile of the standard normal distribution. Note that, to avoid notational clutter, we reuse the notations from Section
3
(e.g.,
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
,
\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)
,
\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})
,
\hat{V}(x_{0})
, and
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
), but we emphasize that these quantities are constructed here without sample-splitting. In practice, the regularization parameter
\lambda
can be selected via a data-driven tuning procedure as detailed in Section
3.5
.

D.2
Theoretical Guarantees

For PPCI without sample-splitting, we establish a nonasymptotic upper bound for the PPCI estimator

\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
in (
17
).

Theorem 4
.

Let
x_{0}
be an interior point of
\mathcal{X}
, and let
\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})
denote the locally unique solution in
\Theta_{0}
to
\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=0
. Suppose Assumptions
1
–
4
hold and the regularization parameter satisfies

\lambda^{-1}=o\left(\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{\log N^{\prime}}\right)^{\frac{m}{d}}\wedge\left(\frac{n\wedge N}{\log N^{\prime}}\right)^{\frac{2m}{d}}\right).

Then for all sufficiently large
n,N
, with probability at least

1-24(N^{\prime})^{-1}
with
N^{\prime}=n+N
,

\displaystyle|\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})|\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{c_{J}}\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}\left(\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{N}}\right)}_{\text{moment estimation error}}

(21)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{32}{c_{J}}\,\kappa\,\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}}_{\text{weight estimation error}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{c_{J}}\,\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\sqrt{\lambda D(x_{0};\lambda)}}_{\text{regularization bias}}.

Moreover, under the optimal choice of

\lambda\asymp\left(\frac{\mathscr{N}}{\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}}\right)^{2}
,
where

\mathscr{N}:=\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}\left(\frac{8\sqrt{2}B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{8\sqrt{2}B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{N}}+\frac{32\kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}}{\sqrt{N^{\prime}}}\right)

collects the stochastic terms from labeled and unlabeled data,
there exists
C_{0}>0
such that

|\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{C_{0}}}{c_{J}}\,\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{d}{2m}}\,\mathscr{N}^{\,1-\frac{d}{2m}}.

(22)

Compared to Theorem
1
, the error bound in (
21
) shares a very similar structure, consisting of the moment estimation error, the weight estimation error, and the regularization bias. The primary distinction lies in the weight estimation error: specifically, it scales at the rate of
(N^{\prime})^{-1/2}
for the non-split estimator, as opposed to
N^{-1/2}
in Theorem
1
. This improvement arises because the non-split procedure fully exploits all available covariates from both the labeled and unlabeled datasets (
N^{\prime}=n+N
) to estimate the empirical localization weight function. Consequently, without the independence afforded by sample-splitting, the proof of Theorem
4
must address additional theoretical challenges to handle the resulting complex dependencies. The empirical localization weight is constructed from pooled covariates and is not independent of the moment terms, creating shared-design dependence. We resolve this using a leave-one-out decoupling argument combined with operator-resolvent bounds and stability-based concentration techniques.

Establishing Theorem
4
involves substantial technical challenges.
The key difficulty is the shared-design dependence between the labeled and unlabeled parts:
the empirical localization weight
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
is learned from the pooled covariates

\{\bar{X}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}
, and is therefore not independent of

\{\ell(Y_{i};\theta)-\ell(f(X_{i});\theta)\}_{i=1}^{n}
nor
\{\ell(f(\tilde{X}_{u});\theta)\}_{u=1}^{N}
.
As a result, controlling the interaction term that couples random objects built from overlapping data is nontrivial.

To decouple this dependence, we employ a LOO analysis
(
Chernozhukov
et al. 2020
;
Chen et al. 2022
)
to isolate the influence of each observation.
Two additional ideas are crucial.
First, we derive a high-probability bound for
\|\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}-w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}

via an operator-resolvent argument in the
(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{1/2}
geometry.
Second, we establish a sharp uniform LOO stability bound for
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}

by exploiting an exact noiseless KRR representation with responses
K(\bar{X}_{j},x_{0})
, which yields uniform control of leverage terms.
Combining these ingredients, we perform an LOO re-centering of the interaction by replacing
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}-w_{x_{0},\lambda}
with its LOO analogue, and then control the remaining dependence via a stability-based concentration argument.

Finally, we compare the lower bound in Theorem
2
with the upper bound implied by (
22
).
Recall that when
n\asymp N
or
N/n\to\infty
, the resulting rate in (
22
) is

O(n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{d}{4m}})
up to a
\log N^{\prime}
factor.
When
n/N\to\infty
, the rate in (
22
) becomes

O(N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{d}{4m}})
up to a
\log N^{\prime}
factor.
In both regimes, the exponent
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{d}{4m}
matches the Sobolev
minimax lower bound exponent
1-\frac{d}{2m}
after squaring the error,
and therefore the upper bound in Theorem
4
is minimax optimal up to the logarithmic factor.

Next, we establish the asymptotic properties of the pointwise estimator

\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
for the conditional target
\theta_{0}(x_{0})
.

Theorem 5
.

Under the settings of Theorem
4
and Assumptions
1
-
5
,
define
J_{\lambda}(x_{0})=J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
and
V(x_{0}):=\frac{1}{n}\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+\frac{1}{N}\sigma^{2}_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
,
where
\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}(\theta)=\mathrm{Var}(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)(\ell(Y;\theta)-\ell(f(X);\theta)))
and
\sigma^{2}_{f}(\theta)=\mathrm{Var}(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,\ell(f(X);\theta))
.
Suppose that

\displaystyle n=o\left(\frac{N}{\log N}\right),\quad\lambda=o(n^{-1}),\quad\lambda^{-1}=o\left(\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{\log N^{\prime}}\right)^{\frac{m}{d}}\wedge\left(\frac{n}{(\log N^{\prime})^{2}}\right)^{\frac{2m}{d}}\right).

(23)

Then, as
n,N\to\infty
,

\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})\big)}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}\ \to\ N(0,1).

To make the scaling requirements in (
23
) transparent, suppose that
N=n^{r_{1}}
and
\lambda=n^{r_{2}}

for some exponents
r_{1}>0
and
r_{2}<0
. Ignoring logarithmic factors that do not change the
polynomial exponents, the conditions in (
23
) are satisfied provided that

r_{1}>1,\quad-\frac{m}{d}\min\{r_{1},2\}\ <\ r_{2}\ <\ -1.

Since
r_{1}>1
ensures that the total sample size
N^{\prime}=n+N
is asymptotically dominated by the unlabeled sample size
N
, substituting
N
with
N^{\prime}
does not alter the dominant polynomial exponents. Consequently, the scaling requirements for
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
remain exactly identical to those in the cross-fitting setting in Section
4
.

Theorem
5
suggests a natural construction of pointwise conditional
confidence intervals at
x_{0}
; the corresponding plug-in procedure is described in
Section (
20
). We now formalize the resulting asymptotic coverage.
In practice, we approximate

J_{\lambda}(x_{0})
by the empirical Jacobian
\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})
in
(
18
), and
V(x_{0})
by
\hat{V}(x_{0})
in (
19
), both evaluated at

\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
. This leads to the confidence interval
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
in (
20
).
The next corollary states consistency of these plug-in quantities and asymptotic coverage.

Corollary 2
.

Under the
setting of Theorem
5
,
\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})\to_{p}J_{\lambda}(x_{0})
and

\hat{V}(x_{0})\to_{p}V(x_{0})
,
where the probability is taken over the labeled and unlabeled samples. Moreover,
for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, as
n,N\to\infty
,

\mathbb{P}(\theta_{0}(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0}))\to 1-\alpha.

D.3
Proofs of Theorems in Appendix
D.2

First, we review our used notation. Let
x_{0}
be an interior point of
\mathcal{X}
.
For
\theta\in\Theta
, define

r(y,x;\theta):=\ell(y;\theta)-\ell(f(x);\theta),\qquad u(x;\theta):=\ell(f(x);\theta).

Let
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be labeled data and
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u=1}^{N}
be
unlabeled covariates, mutually independent, with
X_{i},\tilde{X}_{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\rho_{X}
.
Let
N^{\prime}:=n+N
and let
\{\bar{X}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}
denote the pooled covariates
containing both
\{X_{i}\}
and
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}
.

RKHS operators and population weights.
Recall the covariance operator
T_{K}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
and its empirical version
T_{K}:=\mathbb{E}[K_{X}\otimes K_{X}]
and
\hat{T}_{K}:=\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}K_{\bar{X}_{j}}\otimes K_{\bar{X}_{j}}
.
Fix
\lambda>0
and define the population and empirical localization weights

w_{x_{0},\lambda}:=(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}},\qquad\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}:=(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}},\qquad\Delta w:=\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}-w_{x_{0},\lambda}.

Define leverage and effective dimension

D(x_{0};\lambda):=\langle K_{x_{0}},(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},\qquad D(\lambda):=\mathrm{Tr}\big((T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}T_{K}\big).

RKHS embeddings.
Define the empirical and population RKHS embeddings

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta)\,K_{X_{i}}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)\,K_{\tilde{X}_{u}}\in\mathcal{H},

\displaystyle\mu(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[r(Y,X;\theta)\,K_{X}]+\mathbb{E}[u(X;\theta)\,K_{X}]=\mathbb{E}[\ell(Y;\theta)\,K_{X}]\in\mathcal{H}.

Recall that
\eta(x;\theta):=\mathbb{E}[\ell(Y;\theta)|X=x]\in\mathcal{H}
. Then

\mu(\theta)=\mathbb{E}[\eta(X;\theta)K_{X}]=T_{K}\,\eta(\cdot;\theta)
.

Localized PPI moments.

Define the population localized PPI moment and its empirical estimator:

\displaystyle\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,r(Y,X;\theta)\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,u(X;\theta)\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\ell(Y;\theta)\big],

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\,r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\,u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta).

Let the true target be defined by
\eta(x_{0};\theta_{0}(x_{0}))=0
, and write

\theta_{0}:=\theta_{0}(x_{0})
.
Define the estimator
\hat{\theta}=\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
as any solution to

\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\hat{\theta})=0
.
Define the oracle estimator

\tilde{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\,r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}w_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\,u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta).

Decomposition.

Then, with the definitions of
\hat{\mu}(\theta)
and
\mu(\theta)
, the reproducing
property yields

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\langle w_{x_{0},\lambda},\hat{\mu}(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},\quad\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\langle\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda},\hat{\mu}(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},\quad\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\langle w_{x_{0},\lambda},\mu(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

Hence the exact decomposition

\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)-\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=I_{1}(x_{0};\theta)+I_{2}(x_{0};\theta)+I_{3}(x_{0};\theta),

(24)

where
I_{1}(x_{0};\theta):=\langle w_{x_{0},\lambda},\hat{\mu}(\theta)-\mu(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
,
I_{2}(x_{0};\theta):=\langle\Delta w,\hat{\mu}(\theta)-\mu(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
, and
I_{3}(x_{0};\theta):=\langle\Delta w,\mu(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Regularization bias.
Since
\eta(x_{0};\theta_{0})=0
, we have

\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{0})=\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{0})-\eta(x_{0};\theta_{0})=\big((R_{\lambda}-I)\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0})\big)(x_{0}),

where
R_{\lambda}:=(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}T_{K}
.
Using
R_{\lambda}-I=-\lambda(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}
and Cauchy–Schwarz,

|\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{0})|\leq\sqrt{\lambda}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0})\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

(25)

Jacobians.
Define the population and empirical Jacobians

J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta):=\partial_{\theta}\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,\partial_{\theta}\ell(Y;\theta)\big],

and

\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta):=\partial_{\theta}\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\,\partial_{\theta}r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\,\partial_{\theta}u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta).

Technical lemmas.

Before proceeding to the main proofs of the theorems in Appendix
D
, we first provide three technical lemmas that will be frequently invoked throughout our analysis. Lemma
3
, in particular, plays a foundational role in our theoretical framework. It sharply characterizes the concentration properties of

A(\lambda):=(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1/2}(\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K})(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1/2}.

Lemma 1

(Theorem 3.8 of
Adams and Fournier 2003
)
.

Assume that
x_{0}
lies in the interior of

\mathcal{X}
. Then there exist constants
C_{0}>0
,
R_{0}>0
, and
C_{1}>0
such that

\sup_{\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq R\|v\|_{L_{2}}}\frac{|v(x_{0})|}{\|v\|_{L_{2}}}>C_{0}R^{\frac{d}{2m}},\qquad\text{for all }R\geq R_{0},

and

|v(x_{0})|\leq\|v\|_{\infty}\leq C_{1}\,\|v\|_{L_{2}}^{1-\frac{d}{2m}}\,\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{d}{2m}}.

Lemma 2

(Bounds for
\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
)
.

Under the settings of Proposition
1
and Assumption
4
, there exist constants
0<c_{w}\leq 1
depending only on
(B_{\phi},\rho_{0},\rho_{1},d,m)
such that for all
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0}]
,

c_{w}\,\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)}{\lambda}\ \leq\ \|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\ \leq\ \frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)}{\lambda}.

(26)

Lemma 3
.

For matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
denotes the operator norm. Write
\tau:=\frac{d}{2m}\in(0,1)
.
Then there exists a constant
C_{a}:=C_{1}^{2}\,\tau^{\tau}(1-\tau)^{1-\tau}

such that, for every
\delta\in(0,1]
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\|A(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{4\,C_{a}}{3\,N^{\prime}\,\lambda^{\tau}}\,\log\Big(\frac{4\,\lambda^{\tau}D(\lambda)}{\delta}\Big)+\sqrt{\frac{2\,C_{a}}{N^{\prime}\,\lambda^{\tau}}\,\log\Big(\frac{4\,\lambda^{\tau}D(\lambda)}{\delta}\Big)}.

(27)

Moreover, under Proposition
1
,
D(\lambda)\asymp\lambda^{-\tau}
,
taking
\delta=(N^{\prime})^{-1}
yields

\|A(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \frac{8\,C_{a}}{3\,N^{\prime}\,\lambda^{\tau}}\,\log N^{\prime}+\sqrt{\frac{4\,C_{a}}{N^{\prime}\,\lambda^{\tau}}\,\log N^{\prime}}.

(28)

Let the stability event be
\mathscr{E}:=\{\|A(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1/2\}
. If the regularization satisfies

\lambda\ \geq\ \Big(\frac{256\,C_{a}\,\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}\Big)^{1/\tau}=\Big(\frac{256\,C_{a}\,\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}\Big)^{2m/d},

(29)

we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\geq 1-(N^{\prime})^{-1}
for all sufficiently large
N^{\prime}
.

Bounds for
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
, and
I_{3}
.

In the following, we derive detailed error bounds for the three components in (
24
), showing that
I_{1}
and
I_{3}
constitute the leading terms, whereas
I_{2}
is negligible and can be dominated by
I_{1}
and
I_{3}
. First, we have the following results for
I_{1}
:

Lemma 4

(Oracle sampling error)
.

Under Assumption
1
, for the oracle sampling error
I_{1}(x_{0};\theta):=\tilde{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)-\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)
,
if the regularization level satisfies

\lambda\ \geq\ \Big(\frac{8}{9}\,C_{1}^{2}\,\frac{\log N^{\prime}}{n\wedge N}\Big)^{2m/d},

(30)

then with probability at least
1-8(N^{\prime})^{-1}
,

|I_{1}(x_{0};\theta)|\ \leq\ 2\sqrt{2\log N^{\prime}}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\left(\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta)}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{B_{f}(\theta)}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

(31)

To control
I_{2}
, the main difficulty is that it couples two random objects built from data: the localization weight
w_{x_{0},\lambda}
is learned from the pooled covariates of size
n+N
, while the score averages appearing in
I_{2}
are formed from the labeled sample and the unlabeled sample. This dependence rules out a direct concentration argument and makes
I_{2}
substantially more delicate than the other terms.
To address this challenge, we break the analysis into three steps. We (i) derive a high-probability bound for
\|\Delta w\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}
in Lemma
5
, (ii) establish a LOO prediction-gap bound for the noiseless KRR weight estimator
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
in Lemma
6
, and (iii) combine (i)–(ii) to obtain a clean high-probability interaction bound for
I_{2}
in Lemma
3
.

Lemma 5

(High-probability bound for
\|\Delta w\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}
)
.

Under Assumption
4
and the event
\mathscr{E}
in Lemma
3
. Write
\Delta w:=\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}-w_{x_{0},\lambda}
.
Then there exists an absolute constant
c_{0}>0
such that with probability at least

1-(2N^{\prime})^{-1}
,

\|\Delta w\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}\leq c_{0}\,B_{\phi}\,\sqrt{\frac{D(\lambda)\,D(x_{0};\lambda)\,\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}.

(32)

Lemma 6

(LOO prediction gap)
.

Under Assumption
4
, let
\bar{X}_{1},\dots,\bar{X}_{N^{\prime}}
be the pooled covariates used to form
\hat{T}_{K}
and

\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}=(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}}
.
For each index
j\in\{1,\dots,N^{\prime}\}
, define the rank-one removed operator

\hat{T}_{K}^{(-j)}:=\hat{T}_{K}-\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\,K_{\bar{X}_{j}}\otimes K_{\bar{X}_{j}},\qquad\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}^{(-j)}:=(\hat{T}_{K}^{(-j)}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}}.

Then on
\mathscr{E}
from Lemma
3
, there exists an absolute constant
c>0
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{1\leq j\leq N^{\prime}}\ \big|\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(x)-\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}^{(-j)}(x)\big|\leq c\,\kappa^{2}\,B_{\phi}^{2}\,\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

(33)

Consequently, writing
\Delta w:=\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}-w_{x_{0},\lambda}
and

\Delta w^{(-j)}:=\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}^{(-j)}-w_{x_{0},\lambda}
, we have on
\mathscr{E}
,

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{1\leq j\leq N^{\prime}}\ \big|\Delta w(x)-\Delta w^{(-j)}(x)\big|\leq c\,\kappa^{2}\,B_{\phi}^{2}\,\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

(34)

Lemma 7

(High-probability interaction bound for
I_{2}
)
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
4
, and the event
\mathscr{E}
from Lemma
3
, for
I_{2}(x_{0};\theta):=\big\langle\Delta w,\ \hat{\mu}(\theta)-\mu(\theta)\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
, with probability at least
1-3(2N^{\prime})^{-1}
,

|I_{2}(x_{0};\theta)|\lesssim B_{\phi}D(\lambda)\Big(\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta)}{n}+\frac{B_{f}(\theta)}{N}\Big)\log N^{\prime}+(B_{f}(\theta)+B_{Y-f}(\theta))\kappa^{2}B_{\phi}^{2}\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

(35)

For
I_{3}
, we have the following results:

Lemma 8

(Operator-induced term
I_{3}
)
.

Under Assumption
4
, recall that
I_{3}(x_{0};\theta):=\langle\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}-w_{x_{0},\lambda},\ \mu(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.
On the event
\mathscr{E}
in Lemma
3
, in addition, assume the regularization level satisfies
D(\lambda)\sqrt{\frac{\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}\to 0
.
Then for sufficiently large
N^{\prime}
, with probability at least

1-2(N^{\prime})^{-1}
,

|I_{3}(x_{0};\theta)|\ \leq\ 8\,\kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta)\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}.

Jacobian stability and invertibility.

We analyze the stability and invertibility of the empirical Jacobian.
The proof follows the same decomposition strategy as in bounding
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
, and
I_{3}
, but requires a more careful treatment of the Jacobian-specific terms.

Lemma 9

(Jacobian stability and invertibility)
.

Under Assumptions
2
,
3
, and
4
, suppose that the regularization level satisfies

D(\lambda)\sqrt{\frac{\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}\ \to\ 0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{n\wedge N}\ \to\ 0.

(36)

Then on the event
\mathscr{E}
from
Lemma
3
, for all sufficiently large
N^{\prime}
, with probability at least
1-11.5(N^{\prime})^{-1}

\inf_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)\big|\ \geq\ c_{J}/2

(37)

On
\mathscr{E}
and the same event of probability at least
1-11.5(N^{\prime})^{-1}
,
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)^{-1}\big|\ \leq\ \frac{2}{c_{J}}
.

Proof of Theorem
4
.
With Lemma
9
, we provide the following proof.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

By definition,
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})=(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0}))
.
We bound these two terms separately.

Step 1: linear expansion around
\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})
.

Since
\hat{\theta}\in\Theta_{0}
solves
\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))=0
and

\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})\in\Theta_{0}
solves
\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))=0
, the mean-value theorem yields
the existence of some
\tilde{\theta}
between
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
and
\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})
such that

0=\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))=\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\tilde{\theta})\,(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})),

hence

\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})=-\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\tilde{\theta})^{-1}\,\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})).

(38)

Step 2: control
\hat{J}_{\lambda}^{-1}
on
\Theta_{0}
.

Let
\mathscr{E}:=\{\|A(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1/2\}
be the stability event from
Lemma
3
. By Lemma
9
, on
\mathscr{E}
and for all large
N^{\prime}
,
with probability at least
1-11.5(N^{\prime})^{-1}
,

\inf_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)|\geq c_{J}/2,\qquad\text{and therefore}\qquad\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)^{-1}|\leq 2/c_{J}.

Define the event

\mathscr{E}_{J}:=\Big\{\inf_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)|\geq c_{J}/2\Big\}.

Then, using
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{c})\leq(N^{\prime})^{-1}
from Lemma
3
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{E}_{J})\ \geq\ 1-12.5(N^{\prime})^{-1}.

(39)

On
\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{E}_{J}
, using (
38
),

|\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})|\leq\frac{2}{c_{J}}\,|\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|.

(40)

Step 3: control
\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})
.

Since
\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))=0
, the exact decomposition (
24
) gives

\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))-\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))=I_{1}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})),

hence

|\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|\leq|I_{1}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|+|I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|+|I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|.

(41)

Step 3a: high-probability events.

Let
\mathscr{E}_{I_{1}}
be the event on which Lemma
4
yields (
31
)
(at
\theta=\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})
), let
\mathscr{E}_{I_{2}}
be the event on which Lemma
7

yields (
35
) (on
\mathscr{E}
), and let
\mathscr{E}_{I_{3}}
be the event on which
Lemma
8
yields its bound (on
\mathscr{E}
).
Then for all large
N^{\prime}
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}_{I_{1}}^{c})\leq 8(N^{\prime})^{-1},\qquad\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}_{I_{2}}^{c}|\mathscr{E})\leq 1.5(N^{\prime})^{-1},\qquad\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}_{I_{3}}^{c}|\mathscr{E})\leq 2(N^{\prime})^{-1}.

Combining with (
39
) and a union bound gives the global event

\mathscr{G}:=\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{E}_{J}\cap\mathscr{E}_{I_{1}}\cap\mathscr{E}_{I_{2}}\cap\mathscr{E}_{I_{3}}

satisfying

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{G})\ \geq\ 1-24(N^{\prime})^{-1}.

Step 3b: plug in
I_{1}
and
I_{3}
, and isolate
I_{2}
.

On
\mathscr{G}
, Lemma
4
and Lemma
8
yield

\displaystyle|I_{1}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{2\log N^{\prime}}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\left(\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{N}}\right),

(42)

\displaystyle|I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|

\displaystyle\leq 8\,\kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}.

(43)

Also, on
\mathscr{G}
we have the interaction bound from Lemma
7
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}|I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|&\lesssim B_{\phi}D(\lambda)\Big(\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{n}+\frac{B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{N}\Big)\log N^{\prime}\\
&\quad+(B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})))\kappa^{2}B_{\phi}^{2}\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.\end{split}

(44)

Step 3c:
I_{2}
is dominated by
I_{1}
and
I_{3}
.

Using Proposition
1
(comparability
D(\lambda)\asymp D(x_{0};\lambda)
for small
\lambda
),
there exists
C\geq 1
such that
\sqrt{D(\lambda)/D(x_{0};\lambda)}\leq\sqrt{C}
for all small
\lambda
.
For the first (“
1/n
and
1/N
”) component of (
44
), rewrite

\frac{D(\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{n}=\sqrt{\frac{D(\lambda)}{D(x_{0};\lambda)}}\,\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{n}}\,\frac{\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{n}},

and similarly with
n
replaced by
N
.
Therefore, under
\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{n\wedge N}\to 0
,
the first component of
I_{2}
is

B_{\phi}D(\lambda)\Big(\frac{B_{Y-f}}{n}+\frac{B_{f}}{N}\Big)\log N^{\prime}=o\left(\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{B_{Y-f}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{B_{f}}{\sqrt{N}}\Big)\right),

so it is strictly smaller-order than the leading
I_{1}
scale in (
42
).

For the second (“
1/N^{\prime}
”) component of (
44
), observe that

\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\frac{D(\lambda)}{D(x_{0};\lambda)}}\,\sqrt{\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}\log N^{\prime}}}\,\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}.

Since

D(\lambda)=o(\sqrt{N^{\prime}/\log N^{\prime}})
, we have
D(\lambda)/(N^{\prime}\log N^{\prime})\to 0
and hence

\sqrt{D(\lambda)/(N^{\prime}\log N^{\prime})}\to 0
. Thus the second component of
I_{2}
is

\kappa^{2}B_{\phi}^{2}\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}=o\left(\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}\right),

which is smaller-order than the
I_{3}
scale in (
43
) (up to the multiplicative factor

\kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda})\|_{\mathcal{H}}
).

Combining the two displays above,
I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
is
lower-order than the sum of the
I_{1}
and
I_{3}
contributions in (
42
)–(
43
).
Hence, for all sufficiently large
N^{\prime}
,

|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|+|I_{3}|\ \leq\ 2|I_{1}|+2|I_{3}|\qquad\text{on }\mathscr{G}.

(45)

Step 3d: the stochastic bound.

Combining (
40
), (
41
),
(
42
)–(
43
), and (
45
), we get on
\mathscr{G}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})|&\leq\frac{2}{c_{J}}\big(|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|+|I_{3}|\big)\ \leq\ \frac{4}{c_{J}}\big(|I_{1}|+|I_{3}|\big)\\
&\leq\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{c_{J}}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}\,\left(\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\\
&\qquad\quad+\frac{32}{c_{J}}\,\kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}.\end{split}

(46)

Step 4: bias bound for
\theta_{\lambda}-\theta_{0}
.

Because
\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))=0
and
\theta_{0}\in\Theta_{0}
, a mean-value expansion for
the population map
\eta_{\lambda}
yields the existence of
\bar{\theta}
between

\theta_{0}(x_{0})
and
\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})
such that

0=\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))=\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{0}(x_{0}))+J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\bar{\theta})\,(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})).

By Assumption
3
,
|J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\bar{\theta})|\geq c_{J}
, hence

|\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})|\leq\frac{1}{c_{J}}\,|\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{0}(x_{0}))|.

Finally, by (
25
),

|\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})|\leq\frac{1}{c_{J}}\,\sqrt{\lambda}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

(47)

On the global event
\mathscr{G}
,
combine (
46
) and (
47
) to obtain
(
21
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
5
.
We proceed to prove the asymptotic result in Theorem
5
.
We first establish the following Lemma
10
, which provides a lower bound for the variance. We then give the proof of Theorem
5
.

Lemma 10
.

Under Assumptions
4
and
5
,
let
V(x_{0};\theta):=\frac{1}{n}\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}(\theta)+\frac{1}{N}\sigma^{2}_{f}(\theta)
.
There exist constants
\lambda_{0}\in(0,1)
and
c_{0}>0
independent of
\lambda

such that for all
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0}]
,

V(x_{0};\theta)\ \geq\ \Big(\frac{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\,c_{0}}{2}\Big)\,\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)}{n}.

(48)

Proof of Theorem
5
.

First, we prove

\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big)}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}\ \to\ N(0,1).

(49)

Step 1: local linearization at
\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})
.

As in the upper bound proof (Theorem
4
), a mean-value expansion gives

\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})=-\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\tilde{\theta}(x_{0}))^{-1}\,\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})),

(50)

for some
\tilde{\theta}(x_{0})
between
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})
and
\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})
.
Since
\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))=0
, the decomposition (
24
) yields

\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))=I_{1}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})).

(51)

Step 2: Jacobian stability.

By Lemma
9
and the rate conditions (
23
),

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)-J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)\big|\ \to_{p}\ 0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)^{-1}\big|=O_{p}(1),

where
J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta):=\partial_{\theta}\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)
and

\inf_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)|\geq c_{J}
by Assumption
3
.
In particular, since
\tilde{\theta}(x_{0})\in\Theta_{0}
w.p. tending to one,

\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\tilde{\theta}(x_{0}))^{-1}\ \to_{p}\ J_{\lambda}(x_{0})^{-1}.

(52)

Combining (
50
)–(
51
),

\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})=-\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\tilde{\theta}(x_{0}))^{-1}I_{1}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+R_{n,N},

(53)

where

R_{n,N}:=-\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\tilde{\theta}(x_{0}))^{-1}\{I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\}.

Thus, to prove (
49
), it suffices to show:

\frac{I_{1}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}\to N(0,1),

(54)

and

\frac{I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}=o_{p}(1),\qquad\frac{I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}=o_{p}(1).

(55)

Step 3: Lindeberg–Feller CLT for
I_{1}/\sqrt{V(x_{0})}
.

Define the centered summands

\zeta_{i,n}:=\frac{1}{n}\Big\{w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\,r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))-\mathbb{E}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,r(Y,X;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))]\Big\},\qquad i=1,\dots,n,

and

\zeta_{n+u,N}:=\frac{1}{N}\Big\{w_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\,u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))-\mathbb{E}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,u(X;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))]\Big\},\qquad u=1,\dots,N.

Then
I_{1}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))=\sum_{k=1}^{n+N}\zeta_{k}
is a sum of independent, mean-zero terms and

\sum_{k=1}^{n+N}\mathrm{Var}(\zeta_{k})=\frac{1}{n}\sigma^{2}_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+\frac{1}{N}\sigma^{2}_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))=V(x_{0}).

By the Lindeberg–Feller theorem, it suffices to verify that for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\frac{1}{V(x_{0})}\sum_{k=1}^{n+N}\mathbb{E}\big[\zeta_{k}^{2}\,\mathbbm{1}\{|\zeta_{k}|>\varepsilon\sqrt{V(x_{0})}\}\big]\to 0.

(56)

Under Assumption
4
, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle|w_{x_{0},\lambda}(x)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\big|\langle(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}},\ K_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big|^{2}=\big|\langle K_{x_{0}},\ (T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\langle K_{x_{0}},(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\langle K_{x},(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=D(x_{0};\lambda)\,D(x;\lambda).

(57)

Moreover, in the spectral expansion,
D(x;\lambda)\leq B_{\phi}^{2}\,D(\lambda)
,
so combining with (
57
) yields

\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}\ \leq\ B_{\phi}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)\,D(\lambda)}.

(58)

Under Assumption
1
,

|r(Y,X;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|\leq B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
and
|u(X;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|\leq B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))
.
Hence

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|\zeta_{i,n}|\leq\frac{2\,\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}\,B_{Y-f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{n},\qquad\max_{1\leq u\leq N}|\zeta_{n+u,N}|\leq\frac{2\,\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}\,B_{f}(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{N}.

Therefore, it is enough to show

\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq n+N}|\zeta_{k}|}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}\to 0,

(59)

since (
59
) implies the indicator in (
56
) is eventually zero
uniformly in
k
, yielding (
56
).
By Lemma
10
and
\lambda\to 0
, we have
c(\lambda)\geq c_{0}
for all large
n
,
and therefore

V(x_{0})\geq\Big(\frac{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\,c_{0}}{2}\Big)\,\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)}{n}.

Using (
58
) and Lemma
10
,

\displaystyle\frac{\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|\zeta_{i,n}|}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}/n}{\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)/n}}\ \leq\ \frac{B_{\phi}\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)D(\lambda)}/n}{\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)/n}}=B_{\phi}\,\sqrt{\frac{D(\lambda)}{n}}\ \to\ 0,

\displaystyle\frac{\max_{1\leq u\leq N}|\zeta_{n+u,N}|}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}/N}{\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)/n}}\ \leq\ \frac{B_{\phi}\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)D(\lambda)}/N}{\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)/n}}=B_{\phi}\,\frac{\sqrt{n\,D(\lambda)}}{N}\ \to\ 0,

by the condition (
23
). Thus (
59
) holds,
which proves the Lindeberg condition (
56
) and hence establishes
(
54
).

Step 4:
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
are negligible.

From Lemma
10
, we have
\sqrt{V(x_{0})}\gtrsim\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)/n}
.
By Lemma
7
,

|I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|\ \lesssim\ B_{\phi}\,\sqrt{D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)}\Big(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{N}\Big)\log N^{\prime},

and therefore

\frac{|I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}\lesssim\frac{B_{\phi}\sqrt{D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)}(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{N})\log N^{\prime}}{\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)/n}}=B_{\phi}\,\sqrt{D(\lambda)}\,\log N^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{N}\right)\to 0,

by the condition (
23
). Hence
I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))/\sqrt{V(x_{0})}=o_{p}(1)
.
Using Lemma
8
,

|I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|\ \lesssim_{p}\ \kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}},

and thus

\displaystyle\frac{|I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))|}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p}\kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{D(x_{0};\lambda)}}

\displaystyle=\kappa\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\frac{n\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}\to 0,

by the condition (
23
). Hence
I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))/\sqrt{V(x_{0})}=o_{p}(1)
. Then (
55
) holds.

Step 5: conclude.

Multiply (
53
) by
J_{\lambda}(x_{0})/\sqrt{V(x_{0})}
:

\displaystyle\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big)}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})}{\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\tilde{\theta}(x_{0}))}\cdot\frac{I_{1}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}

\displaystyle-\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})}{\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\tilde{\theta}(x_{0}))}\cdot\frac{I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}.

By (
52
),
\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})}{\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\tilde{\theta}(x_{0}))}\to_{p}1
.
By (
54
),
\frac{I_{1}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}\to N(0,1)
.
By (
55
),
\frac{I_{2}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))+I_{3}(x_{0};\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}=o_{p}(1)
.
Slutsky’s theorem yields (
49
).

Step 6: bias is negligible.

By the population bias bound (
25
) in Theorem
4
,

|\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{c_{J}}\,\|\eta(\cdot;\theta_{0}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\sqrt{\lambda D(x_{0};\lambda)}.

Divide by the standard error scale
\sqrt{V(x_{0})}/|J_{\lambda}(x_{0})|
and use Lemma
10
:

\frac{|J_{\lambda}(x_{0})|\,|\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})|}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}\ \lesssim\ \frac{\sqrt{\lambda D(x_{0};\lambda)}}{\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)/n}}=\sqrt{n\lambda}\ \to\ 0.

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})\big)}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big)}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}+\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big(\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})\big)}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{J_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big)}{\sqrt{V(x_{0})}}+o(1).

Combining with (
49
) concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
2
.
We also provide the following proof.

Proof of Corollary
2
.

All probabilities and expectations below are taken over the samples, with
x_{0}
treated as fixed.
The proof proceeds in two steps.

Step 1: consistency of the plug-in Jacobian and variance.

Recall that

\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0}):=\partial_{\theta}\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)\big|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}(x_{0})},\qquad J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta):=\partial_{\theta}\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta).

Let
\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta):=\partial_{\theta}\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)
. By the same
empirical-process argument used to establish Theorem
4
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)-J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)\big|\ \to_{p}\ 0.

Since
\hat{\theta}(x_{0})\to_{p}\theta_{0}(x_{0})
and
\theta\mapsto J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)
is continuous on

\Theta_{0}
by Assumption
2
, it follows that

\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})=\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\hat{\theta}(x_{0}))\ \to_{p}\ J_{\lambda}\big(x_{0}\big).

Moreover, by Assumption
3
,
J_{\lambda}(x_{0})
is bounded away
from zero, hence
\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})
is nonsingular with probability tending to one.

For the variance estimator, under Assumptions
1
–
2
and the consistency of

\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
from the analysis in Theorem
4
together with

\hat{\theta}(x_{0})\to_{p}\theta_{0}(x_{0})
, a law of large numbers implies
\hat{V}(x_{0})\to_{p}V(x_{0})
.

Step 2: asymptotic coverage.

Define the studentized statistic

T_{n,N}(x_{0}):=\frac{\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})\big(\hat{\theta}(x_{0})-\theta_{0}(x_{0})\big)}{\sqrt{\hat{V}(x_{0})}}.

By Step 1,
\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0})\to_{p}J_{\lambda}(x_{0})
and

\hat{V}(x_{0})\to_{p}V(x_{0})
, so Slutsky’s theorem yields

T_{n,N}(x_{0})\to N(0,1)
. The event
\{\theta_{0}(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\}
is equivalent to

\{|T_{n,N}(x_{0})|\leq z_{1-\alpha/2}\}
, hence

\mathbb{P}\big(\theta_{0}(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(|T_{n,N}(x_{0})|\leq z_{1-\alpha/2}\big)\ \to\ 1-\alpha.

This proves the corollary.
∎

D.4
Proofs of Technical Lemmas in Appendix
D.3

Proof of Lemma
2
.

Step 1: spectral representation.

Let
\{\mu_{j},\phi_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
be the eigenvalues and orthonormal eigenfunctions of
T_{K}
. The kernel section
K_{x_{0}}
admits the expansion
K_{x_{0}}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mu_{j}\phi_{j}(x_{0})\phi_{j}
in
\mathcal{H}
. By the spectral theorem,

\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{j}}{(\mu_{j}+\lambda)^{2}}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}.

(60)

Step 2: upper bound.

Since
\mu_{j}+\lambda\geq\lambda
for all
j\geq 1
, we have
\frac{1}{(\mu_{j}+\lambda)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda(\mu_{j}+\lambda)}
. Substituting this into (
60
) yields:

\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{j}}{\mu_{j}+\lambda}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}=\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)}{\lambda}.

Step 3: lower bound.

From Proposition
1
, we have
D(x_{0};\lambda)\asymp\lambda^{-d/2m}
. To prove the lower bound, we fix a constant
c>1
and define the band index set
\mathcal{J}(\lambda):=\{j:\lambda\leq\mu_{j}\leq c\lambda\}
. For
j\in\mathcal{J}(\lambda)
, the termwise denominator satisfies
(\mu_{j}+\lambda)^{2}\leq(c+1)^{2}\lambda^{2}
. Thus,

\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\geq\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\frac{\mu_{j}}{(\mu_{j}+\lambda)^{2}}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}\geq\frac{1}{(c+1)^{2}\lambda}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}.

(61)

We write the band mass as the difference between the head mass and the tail mass:

\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}=\sum_{\mu_{j}\geq\lambda}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}-\sum_{\mu_{j}>c\lambda}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}.

For the head mass, observe that
D(x_{0};\lambda)=\sum\frac{\mu_{j}}{\mu_{j}+\lambda}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}
. Since
\frac{\mu_{j}}{\mu_{j}+\lambda}\leq 1
when
\mu_{j}\geq\lambda
and
\frac{\mu_{j}}{\mu_{j}+\lambda}<\frac{\mu_{j}}{\lambda}
when
\mu_{j}<\lambda
, we have:

\sum_{\mu_{j}\geq\lambda}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}\geq D(x_{0};\lambda)-\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{\mu_{j}<\lambda}\mu_{j}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}\geq c_{1}\lambda^{-d/2m}-c_{2}B_{\phi}^{2}\lambda^{1-d/2m}\frac{1}{\lambda},

where we use
\mu_{j}\asymp j^{-2m/d}

Fischer and Steinwart 2020
. For small
\lambda
, this implies
\sum_{\mu_{j}\geq\lambda}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}\geq\frac{c_{1}}{2}\lambda^{-d/2m}
.
For the tail mass, using the upper bound from Step 2 of Proposition
1
:

\sum_{\mu_{j}>c\lambda}\phi_{j}(x_{0})^{2}\leq 2D(x_{0};c\lambda)\leq 2C(c\lambda)^{-d/2m}.

By choosing
c
large enough such that
2Cc^{-d/2m}\leq c_{1}/4
, we ensure the band mass is at least
\frac{c_{1}}{4}\lambda^{-d/2m}
.
Substituting this into (
61
) and noting
\lambda^{-d/2m}\asymp D(x_{0};\lambda)
concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3
.

Step 1: rewrite
A(\lambda)
.

Define the preconditioned feature map

\psi_{\lambda}(x)\ :=\ (T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1/2}K_{x}\ \in\ \mathcal{H},\qquad K_{x}:=K(x,\cdot).

Then, using
T_{K}=\mathbb{E}[K_{X}\otimes K_{X}]
and
\hat{T}_{K}=\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}K_{X_{i}}\otimes K_{X_{i}}
,

\displaystyle(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1/2}\hat{T}_{K}(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}\psi_{\lambda}(X_{i})\otimes\psi_{\lambda}(X_{i}),

\displaystyle(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1/2}T_{K}(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[\psi_{\lambda}(X)\otimes\psi_{\lambda}(X)\big].

Therefore

\|A(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\Big\|\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}\psi_{\lambda}(X_{i})\otimes\psi_{\lambda}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\lambda}\otimes\psi_{\lambda}]\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\Big\|\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}Z_{i}\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}},

(62)

where the summands are i.i.d. centered self-adjoint operators

Z_{i}:=\psi_{\lambda}(X_{i})\otimes\psi_{\lambda}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\lambda}(X)\otimes\psi_{\lambda}(X)],\qquad\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}]=0.

Step 2: uniform bound.

For each
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\|\psi_{\lambda}(x)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\big\langle K_{x},(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=:D(x;\lambda).

In particular,

\displaystyle\|\psi_{\lambda}(x)\otimes\psi_{\lambda}(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|\psi_{\lambda}(x)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=D(x;\lambda),

\displaystyle\|Z_{i}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\psi_{\lambda}(X_{i})\otimes\psi_{\lambda}(X_{i})\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\lambda}\otimes\psi_{\lambda}]\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Since
\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\lambda}\otimes\psi_{\lambda}]=T_{K}(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}\preceq I
,
we may bound
\|\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\lambda}\otimes\psi_{\lambda}]\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1\leq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}D(x;\lambda)

for all sufficiently small
\lambda
, and hence

\|Z_{i}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ 2\,\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}D(x;\lambda).

(63)

Thus, to apply a Bernstein-type inequality to (
62
),
it remains to control
\sup_{x}D(x;\lambda)
sharply.

Step 3: upper bound for
\sup_{x}D(x;\lambda)
.

Using the variational characterization of
D(x;\lambda)
,
one has for each fixed
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle D(x;\lambda)=\sup_{v\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\langle K_{x},v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}{\langle v,(T_{K}+\lambda I)v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}=\sup_{v\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{v(x)^{2}}{\|v\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}+\lambda\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}.

(64)

By Lemma
1
,
for every
v\in\mathcal{H}
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

|v(x)|^{2}\ \leq\ C_{1}^{2}\,\|v\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2(1-\tau)}\,\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2\tau}.

(65)

Write
a:=\|v\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}
and
b:=\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
. Then (
65
) gives

v(x)^{2}\leq C_{1}^{2}\,a^{1-\tau}b^{\tau}
, and (
64
) implies

D(x;\lambda)\ \leq\ C_{1}^{2}\,\sup_{a>0,b>0}\frac{a^{1-\tau}b^{\tau}}{a+\lambda b}.

(66)

To evaluate the supremum in (
66
), we can normalize the denominator by setting
a+\lambda b=1
, yielding

\sup_{a>0,b>0}\frac{a^{1-\tau}b^{\tau}}{a+\lambda b}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>0,b>0\\
a+\lambda b=1\end{subarray}}a^{1-\tau}b^{\tau}.

Substituting
b=(1-a)/\lambda
for
a\in(0,1)
, we maximize the one-dimensional function:

\phi(a):=a^{1-\tau}\Big(\frac{1-a}{\lambda}\Big)^{\tau}=\lambda^{-\tau}\,a^{1-\tau}(1-a)^{\tau}.

Taking the derivative of
\log\phi(a)
yields:

\frac{d}{da}\log\phi(a)=\frac{1-\tau}{a}-\frac{\tau}{1-a}=0\quad\implies\quad a^{\star}=1-\tau.

Hence,

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>0,b>0\\
a+\lambda b=1\end{subarray}}a^{1-\tau}b^{\tau}=\lambda^{-\tau}\,(1-\tau)^{1-\tau}\tau^{\tau}.

(67)

Combining (
66
) and (
67
) yields the uniform leverage-score bound:

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}D(x;\lambda)\ \leq\ C_{1}^{2}\,\tau^{\tau}(1-\tau)^{1-\tau}\,\lambda^{-\tau}\ =\ C_{a}\,\lambda^{-\tau}.

(68)

Step 4: apply Bernstein inequality.

We now plug (
68
) into a standard Bernstein inequality for sums of
independent self-adjoint random operators.

First, (
63
) and (
68
) give the uniform almost sure bound

\|Z_{i}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ 2\,C_{a}\,\lambda^{-\tau}.

Second, define the second-moment operator

\Sigma_{\lambda}:=\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\lambda}(X)\otimes\psi_{\lambda}(X)]=(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1/2}T_{K}(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1/2}.

Its trace equals the effective dimension:

\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma_{\lambda})=\mathrm{Tr}\big(T_{K}(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}\big)=D(\lambda).

Then for any
\delta\in(0,1]
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\Big\|\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}Z_{i}\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \frac{4\,\sup_{x}D(x;\lambda)}{3N^{\prime}}\,\log\Big(\frac{4\,D(\lambda)}{\delta}\Big)+\sqrt{\frac{2\,\sup_{x}D(x;\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}\,\log\Big(\frac{4\,D(\lambda)}{\delta}\Big)}.

We now replace
\sup_{x}D(x;\lambda)
by (
68
).
Since our bound scales as
\lambda^{-\tau}
, it is convenient
to rewrite the logarithmic factor in terms of
\lambda^{\tau}D(\lambda)
by absorbing the
\lambda^{-\tau}
inside the logarithm (adjusting universal constants appropriately), which yields exactly (
27
).

Step 5: specialize to
\delta=(N^{\prime})^{-1}
.

Take
\delta=(N^{\prime})^{-1}
so the confidence is
1-(N^{\prime})^{-1}
. Let

L_{\lambda}:=\log\Big(\frac{4\lambda^{\tau}D(\lambda)}{\delta}\Big)=\log\Big(4\lambda^{\tau}D(\lambda)\,N^{\prime}\Big).

Under
D(\lambda)\leq C_{D}\lambda^{-\tau}
(Proposition
1
), we have
\lambda^{\tau}D(\lambda)\leq C_{D}
. Thus,

L_{\lambda}\leq\log(4C_{D}N^{\prime})=\log(4C_{D})+\log N^{\prime}.

For all sufficiently large
N^{\prime}
(such that
N^{\prime}\geq 4C_{D}
), we have
L_{\lambda}\leq 2\log N^{\prime}
.
Plugging this upper bound into (
27
) directly yields (
28
).

Step 6: ensure the right-hand side is
<1/2
.

Let
U:=\frac{C_{a}\,L_{\lambda}}{N^{\prime}\lambda^{\tau}}
. Then (
27
) reads
\|A(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \frac{4}{3}U+\sqrt{2U}
.
To ensure
\|A(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1/2
, it suffices to require
U\leq 1/128
(since
\frac{4}{3}(\frac{1}{128})+\sqrt{\frac{2}{128}}<1/2
).
Using
L_{\lambda}\leq 2\log N^{\prime}
from Step 5, the condition
U\leq 1/128
is implied by

\frac{C_{a}\,(2\log N^{\prime})}{N^{\prime}\lambda^{\tau}}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{128},\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad N^{\prime}\lambda^{\tau}\ \geq\ 256\,C_{a}\,\log N^{\prime},

which is exactly (
29
). Under (
29
),
we obtain that the event
\mathscr{E}
holds with probability at least
1-(N^{\prime})^{-1}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4
.

Write

\displaystyle I_{1}(x_{0};\theta)=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta)-\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)r(Y,X;\theta)\big]\Big)}_{=:U_{n}(x_{0};\theta)}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\Big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)-\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)u(X;\theta)\big]\Big)}_{=:U_{N}(x_{0};\theta)}.

Step 1: bound for
\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}
.
Using the spectral decomposition of
T_{K}
, one verifies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)^{2}\big]=\big\langle w_{x_{0},\lambda},T_{K}w_{x_{0},\lambda}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\big\langle K_{x_{0}},(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=D(x_{0};\lambda).

(69)

By Lemma
2
,
\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\frac{D(x_{0};\lambda)}{\lambda}
.
Moreover, by (
69
),

\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{L_{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)^{2}\big]\leq D(x_{0};\lambda).

Applying Lemma
1
to
v=w_{x_{0},\lambda}
and combining the two bounds above yields
the sharper deterministic sup-norm control

\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{1}\,\Big(\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\Big)^{1-\frac{d}{2m}}\Big(\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)/\lambda}\Big)^{\frac{d}{2m}}=C_{1}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\lambda^{-\frac{d}{4m}}.

(70)

Step 2: Bernstein for
U_{n}
.

Recall that

Z_{R,i}:=w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\,r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta)-\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,r(Y,X;\theta)\big],\qquad i=1,\dots,n,

so that
U_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{R,i}
and
\mathbb{E}[Z_{R,i}]=0
.
By Assumption
1
,
|r(Y,X;\theta)|\leq B_{Y-f}(\theta)
almost surely, hence by (
70
),

|Z_{R,i}|\leq 2\,\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}\,B_{Y-f}(\theta)\leq 2\,C_{1}\,B_{Y-f}(\theta)\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\lambda^{-\frac{d}{4m}}.

Moreover, exactly as before,

\mathrm{Var}(Z_{R,1})=\mathrm{Var}\big(w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,r(Y,X;\theta)\big)\leq B_{Y-f}^{2}(\theta)\,\mathbb{E}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)^{2}]\leq B_{Y-f}^{2}(\theta)\,D(x_{0};\lambda).

Applying Bernstein’s inequality to
\{Z_{R,i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
gives: with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}|U_{n}|&\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\,\mathrm{Var}(Z_{R,1})\,\log(2/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{2\,\|Z_{R,1}\|_{\infty}\,\log(2/\delta)}{3n}\\
&\leq B_{Y-f}(\theta)\sqrt{\frac{2\,D(x_{0};\lambda)\,\log(4/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{4}{3}\,B_{Y-f}(\theta)\,\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}\,\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{n}\\
&\leq B_{Y-f}(\theta)\sqrt{\frac{2\,D(x_{0};\lambda)\,\log(4/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{4}{3}\,C_{1}\,B_{Y-f}(\theta)\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\lambda^{-\frac{d}{4m}}\,\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{n}.\end{split}

(71)

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and suppose the regularization level satisfies (
30
).
Using
n\wedge N\leq n
,

\lambda^{d/(2m)}\ \geq\ \frac{8}{9}\,C_{1}^{2}\,\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{n}\quad\to\quad\frac{4}{3}\,C_{1}\,\lambda^{-\frac{d}{4m}}\,\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{n}\ \leq\ \sqrt{\frac{2\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

Substituting this into (
71
) yields that, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

|U_{n}|\leq 2\,B_{Y-f}(\theta)\sqrt{\frac{2\,D(x_{0};\lambda)\,\log(4/\delta)}{n}}=2\sqrt{2\log(4/\delta)}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

(72)

Step 3: Bernstein for
U_{N}
.

Define i.i.d. centered summands

Z_{U,u}:=w_{x_{0},\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{u})\,u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)-\mathbb{E}\big[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)\,u(X;\theta)\big],\qquad u=1,\dots,N,

so that
U_{N}=N^{-1}\sum_{u=1}^{N}Z_{U,u}
and
\mathbb{E}[Z_{U,u}]=0
.
By Assumption
1
,
|u(X;\theta)|\leq B_{f}(\theta)
, and by (
70
),

|Z_{U,u}|\leq 2\,\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}\,B_{f}(\theta)\leq 2\,C_{1}\,B_{f}(\theta)\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\lambda^{-\frac{d}{4m}},\quad\mathrm{Var}(Z_{U,1})\leq B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,D(x_{0};\lambda).

Repeating the Bernstein argument from Step 2 and using again (
30
) with
n\wedge N\leq N
gives: with probability at least
1-\delta
,

|U_{N}|\leq 2\sqrt{2\log(4/\delta)}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\frac{B_{f}(\theta)}{\sqrt{N}}.

(73)

Step 4.

By independence and a union bound, with probability at least
1-2\delta
,

|I_{1}|\leq|U_{n}|+|U_{N}|\leq 2\sqrt{2\log(4/\delta)}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\left(\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta)}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{B_{f}(\theta)}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

In particular, taking
\delta=4(N^{\prime})^{-1}
yields
\log(4/\delta)=\log N^{\prime}
and a bound holding with probability at least

1-8(N^{\prime})^{-1}
,

|I_{1}|\leq 2\sqrt{2\log N^{\prime}}\,\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\left(\frac{B_{Y-f}(\theta)}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{B_{f}(\theta)}{\sqrt{N}}\right),

under the condition (
30
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
5
.

Step 1: compare
\|\Delta w\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}
to a
T_{\lambda}^{1/2}
-norm.

Using
\hat{w}=\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}K_{x_{0}}
and
w=T_{\lambda}^{-1}K_{x_{0}}
,
the resolvent identity gives the exact equality

\Delta w=\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}K_{x_{0}}-T_{\lambda}^{-1}K_{x_{0}}=-\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K})\,T_{\lambda}^{-1}K_{x_{0}}=-\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K})\,w.

Recall that for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\|h\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[h(X)^{2}]=\langle h,T_{K}h\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Since
0\preceq T_{K}\preceq T_{\lambda}:=T_{K}+\lambda I
, we have

\|h\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}=\langle h,T_{K}h\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\langle h,T_{\lambda}h\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\|T_{\lambda}^{1/2}h\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

Applying this to
h=\Delta w
yields

\|\Delta w\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}\leq\|T_{\lambda}^{1/2}\Delta w\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

(74)

Step 2: on
\mathscr{E}
, control the resolvent factor.

On
\mathscr{E}
, write

\hat{T}_{\lambda}=T_{\lambda}+\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K}=T_{\lambda}^{1/2}\Big(I+T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}(\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K})T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}\Big)T_{\lambda}^{1/2}=T_{\lambda}^{1/2}(I-A(\lambda))T_{\lambda}^{1/2}.

The condition
\|A(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1/2
implies

\|(I-A(\lambda))^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2
. Hence

T_{\lambda}^{1/2}\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}T_{\lambda}^{1/2}=(I-A(\lambda))^{-1},\qquad\|T_{\lambda}^{1/2}\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}T_{\lambda}^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2.

Combining with Step 1, on
\mathscr{E}
we obtain

\displaystyle\|T_{\lambda}^{1/2}\Delta w\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\big\|T_{\lambda}^{1/2}\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K})w\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\big\|(T_{\lambda}^{1/2}\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}T_{\lambda}^{1/2})\cdot T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}(\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K})w\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\big\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}(\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K})w\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

Together with (
74
), this yields on
\mathscr{E}
:

\|\Delta w\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}\leq 2\,\big\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}(\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K})w\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

(75)

Step 3: reduce to a Hilbert-space average.

Define the mean-zero
\mathcal{H}
-valued random element

\zeta(X):=T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}\Big((K_{X}\otimes K_{X})-T_{K}\Big)w=T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}\Big(K_{X}w(X)-T_{K}w\Big).

Then

T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}(\hat{T}_{K}-T_{K})w=\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\zeta(\bar{X}_{j}),

and therefore, on
\mathscr{E}
,

\|\Delta w\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}\leq 2\Big\|\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\zeta(\bar{X}_{j})\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

(76)

Thus it suffices to control the Hilbert-space average

N^{\prime-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\zeta(\bar{X}_{j})
by a Hilbert-space Bernstein inequality.
The two required inputs are (i) a variance proxy
\mathbb{E}\|\zeta(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

and (ii) a uniform bound on
\|\zeta(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Step 4: variance proxy.

Let
\Delta K:=K_{X}\otimes K_{X}-T_{K}
for
X\sim\rho_{X}
. Then

\mathbb{E}\|\zeta(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\Delta Kw,\ T_{\lambda}^{-1}\Delta Kw\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

Let
a:=K_{X}w(X)\in\mathcal{H}
and
b:=T_{K}w=\mathbb{E}[a]\in\mathcal{H}
. Then
\Delta Kw=a-b
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\Delta Kw,\ T_{\lambda}^{-1}\Delta Kw\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\langle a-b,\ T_{\lambda}^{-1}(a-b)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\langle a,T_{\lambda}^{-1}a\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle b,T_{\lambda}^{-1}b\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\langle a,T_{\lambda}^{-1}a\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

where we dropped the nonnegative term
\langle b,T_{\lambda}^{-1}b\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.
By reproducing,

\displaystyle\langle a,T_{\lambda}^{-1}a\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\langle K_{X}w(X),\ T_{\lambda}^{-1}K_{X}w(X)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=w(X)^{2}\,\langle K_{X},\ T_{\lambda}^{-1}K_{X}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=w(X)^{2}\,D(X;\lambda).

Therefore,
\mathbb{E}\|\zeta(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[w(X)^{2}\,D(X;\lambda)]
. Under Assumption
4
,

D(X;\lambda)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{j}}{\mu_{j}+\lambda}\phi_{j}(X)^{2}\leq\Big(\sup_{j\geq 1}\|\phi_{j}\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{j}}{\mu_{j}+\lambda}\leq B_{\phi}^{2}\,D(\lambda),

hence
\mathbb{E}\|\zeta(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)\mathbb{E}[w(X)^{2}]
.
Finally, since
\mathbb{E}[w(X)^{2}]=\langle w,T_{K}w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\langle w,T_{\lambda}w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
and

T_{\lambda}w=K_{x_{0}}
, we get
\mathbb{E}[w(X)^{2}]\leq\langle w,K_{x_{0}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=w(x_{0})=D(x_{0};\lambda)
.
Combining yields

\mathbb{E}\|\zeta(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq B_{\phi}^{2}\,D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda).

(77)

Step 5: uniform bound.

By triangle inequality,

\|\zeta(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}(K_{X}w(X)-T_{K}w)\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}K_{X}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,|w(X)|+\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}T_{K}w\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

First,

\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}K_{X}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\langle K_{X},\ T_{\lambda}^{-1}K_{X}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=D(X;\lambda)\leq B_{\phi}^{2}\,D(\lambda).

Also,

\displaystyle|w(X)|

\displaystyle=|\langle w,K_{X}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|=|\langle T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}K_{x_{0}},\ T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}K_{X}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}K_{x_{0}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}K_{X}\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,\sqrt{D(X;\lambda)}.

Thus

\displaystyle\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}K_{X}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,|w(X)|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{D(X;\lambda)}\cdot\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\cdot\sqrt{D(X;\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,D(X;\lambda)\leq\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\,B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda).

For the second term, use
0\preceq T_{K}\preceq T_{\lambda}
, implying

\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}T_{K}T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
. Hence

\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}T_{K}w\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\|(T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}T_{K}T_{\lambda}^{-1/2})(T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}K_{x_{0}})\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|T_{\lambda}^{-1/2}K_{x_{0}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}.

Putting together, for all
X
,

\|\zeta(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\big(B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)+1\big).

(78)

Step 6: Hilbert-space Bernstein.

By (
77
) and (
78
), a Bernstein inequality for sums of independent, bounded Hilbert-space random elements
then yields: for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\Big\|\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\zeta(\bar{X}_{j})\Big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq c\left[\sqrt{\frac{B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)\,\log(1/\delta)}{N^{\prime}}}+\frac{\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}(B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)+1)\,\log(1/\delta)}{N^{\prime}}\right],

(79)

for a universal constant
c>0
.
Choose
\delta=1/(2N^{\prime})
in (
79
); then

\log(1/\delta)\asymp\log N^{\prime}
. On the intersection
\mathscr{E}\cap\{\eqref{eq:zeta-bernstein-L2}\text{ holds}\}
,
by (
76
),

\|\Delta w\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}\leq c^{\prime}\left[B_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)\,\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}+\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\frac{(B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)+1)\,\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}\right]

for another universal constant
c^{\prime}>0
.
Under
\lambda\geq C(\log N^{\prime}/N^{\prime})^{2m/d}
,
the second term is dominated by the first, so after adjusting constants we obtain
(
32
).
Finally, on event
\mathscr{E}
, the Bernstein failure (with
\delta=1/(2N^{\prime})
) is
\leq 1/(2N^{\prime})
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
6
.

We isolate the argument in several steps, following the noiseless KRR route.

Step 1: noiseless KRR representation.

Let
y_{j}:=K(\bar{X}_{j},x_{0})
for
j=1,\dots,N^{\prime}
.
Consider the noiseless KRR problem

\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}\in\arg\min_{v\in\mathcal{H}}\left\{\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\big(y_{j}-v(\bar{X}_{j})\big)^{2}+\lambda\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right\}.

(80)

The first-order condition is
(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}=K_{x_{0}}
,
hence the solution of (
80
) coincides with

\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}=(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}}
.
Similarly,
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}^{(-j)}
coincides with the same problem after removing the
single term corresponding to
\bar{X}_{j}
with the fixed
1/N^{\prime}
scaling.

Step 2: leverage and empirical effective dimension.

Let
K\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}\times N^{\prime}}
be the Gram matrix
K_{ij}:=K(\bar{X}_{i},\bar{X}_{j})
and
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{N^{\prime}})^{\top}
.
By representer theorem,
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\cdot)=\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\alpha_{j}K(\bar{X}_{j},\cdot)
with
(K+N^{\prime}\lambda I)\alpha=y
.
The fitted values at the training points satisfy

\hat{y}:=\big(\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\bar{X}_{1}),\dots,\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\bar{X}_{N^{\prime}})\big)^{\top}=K\alpha=K(K+N^{\prime}\lambda I)^{-1}y=:Hy,\quad H:=K(K+N^{\prime}\lambda I)^{-1}.

Define
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{H}
by

\Phi a:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{\prime}}}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}a_{j}K_{\bar{X}_{j}},\qquad a\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}.

Then
\hat{T}_{K}=\Phi\Phi^{\ast}
and
\Phi^{\ast}\Phi=K/N^{\prime}
.
Using resolvent identity,

\Phi^{\ast}(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}\Phi=K(K+N^{\prime}\lambda I)^{-1}=H.

Taking the
(j,j)
entry yields

H_{jj}=\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\big\langle K_{\bar{X}_{j}},(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{\bar{X}_{j}}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=:\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\,\hat{D}(\bar{X}_{j};\lambda).

(81)

Likewise, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, define the cross-leverage

h_{j}(x):=\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\big\langle K_{x},(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{\bar{X}_{j}}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

(82)

Then
h_{j}(\bar{X}_{i})=H_{ij}
for all
i,j
.

Step 3: uniform leverage bounds on
\mathscr{E}
.

On
\mathscr{E}
, the same argument as in Lemma
5
gives

(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}\preceq 2(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}
.
Therefore for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\hat{D}(x;\lambda)=\langle K_{x},(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq 2\langle K_{x},(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=2D(x;\lambda)\leq 2B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda),

where
D(x;\lambda)\leq B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)
follows from Assumption
4
.
Combining with (
81
) yields on
\mathscr{E}
,

\max_{1\leq j\leq N^{\prime}}H_{jj}\leq\frac{2B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

Under
\lambda\geq C(\log N^{\prime}/N^{\prime})^{2m/d}
from Lemma
3
, the regime implies
D(\lambda)=o(N^{\prime})
,
so for
N^{\prime}
large we may ensure

\max_{1\leq j\leq N^{\prime}}H_{jj}\leq\frac{1}{2},\qquad\to\qquad\frac{1}{1-H_{jj}}\leq 2.

(83)

Finally, by Cauchy–Schwarz in
\mathcal{H}
,

|h_{j}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sqrt{\hat{D}(x;\lambda)\,\hat{D}(\bar{X}_{j};\lambda)}\leq\frac{2B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

(84)

Step 4: uniform residual bound.

Let
v=K_{x_{0}}\in\mathcal{H}
. Then
v(\bar{X}_{j})=y_{j}
and
\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=K(x_{0},x_{0})\leq\kappa^{2}
.
By optimality of
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}
in (
80
),

\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}(y_{j}-\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\bar{X}_{j}))^{2}+\lambda\|\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\lambda\|K_{x_{0}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\lambda\kappa^{2}.

In particular,
\|\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\kappa^{2}
, hence for any
x
,

|\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(x)|=|\langle\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda},K_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|\leq\|\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\|K_{x}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\kappa\cdot\sqrt{K(x,x)}\leq\kappa^{2}.

Also
|y_{j}|=|K(\bar{X}_{j},x_{0})|\leq\sqrt{K(\bar{X}_{j},\bar{X}_{j})K(x_{0},x_{0})}\leq\kappa^{2}
.
Therefore for all
j
,

|y_{j}-\hat{y}_{j}|=|K(\bar{X}_{j},x_{0})-\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(\bar{X}_{j})|\leq 2\kappa^{2}.

(85)

Step 5: LOO identity and pointwise prediction gap.

Let
\hat{y}=Hy
be the full fitted vector. Let
\hat{y}^{(-j)}
be the fitted value at
\bar{X}_{j}

produced by the LOO solution that removes the
j
-th observation with the fixed
1/N^{\prime}
scaling.
A standard Schur complement computation yields the exact identity

\hat{y}_{j}-\hat{y}_{j}^{(-j)}=\frac{H_{jj}}{1-H_{jj}}\,(y_{j}-\hat{y}_{j}).

More generally, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(x)-\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}^{(-j)}(x)=\frac{h_{j}(x)}{1-H_{jj}}\,(y_{j}-\hat{y}_{j}),

(86)

where
h_{j}(x)
is as in (
82
). Combine (
86
) with (
83
), (
84
), and (
85
).
On
\mathscr{E}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\big|\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(x)-\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}^{(-j)}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{|h_{j}(x)|}{1-H_{jj}}\ \sup_{1\leq i\leq N^{\prime}}|y_{i}-\hat{y}_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|h_{j}(x)|\cdot 2\kappa^{2}\leq 4\kappa^{2}\cdot\frac{2B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}=\frac{8\kappa^{2}B_{\phi}^{2}D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

This proves (
33
) after renaming the absolute constant. Since
w_{x_{0},\lambda}
is population and hence unchanged by LOO, (
34
) follows immediately.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7
.

Throughout the proof we work on
\mathscr{E}
.

Step 1: decomposition of
\hat{\mu}-\mu
.

Recall the decomposition

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}(\theta)-\mu(\theta)=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta)K_{X_{i}}-\mathbb{E}[r(Y,X;\theta)K_{X}]\Big)

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)K_{\tilde{X}_{u}}-\mathbb{E}[u(X;\theta)K_{X}]\Big).

Therefore

I_{2}(x_{0};\theta)=:I_{2}^{(R)}(x_{0};\theta)+I_{2}^{(U)}(x_{0};\theta),

where

\displaystyle I_{2}^{(R)}(x_{0};\theta)

\displaystyle:=\Big\langle\Delta w,\ \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta)\,K_{X_{i}}-\mathbb{E}[r(Y,X;\theta)K_{X}]\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

(87)

\displaystyle I_{2}^{(U)}(x_{0};\theta)

\displaystyle:=\Big\langle\Delta w,\ \frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)\,K_{\tilde{X}_{u}}-\mathbb{E}[u(X;\theta)K_{X}]\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

(88)

We bound
|I_{2}^{(R)}|
and
|I_{2}^{(U)}|
separately on a common high-probability event.

Part A: unlabeled interaction
I_{2}^{(U)}
.

Step A.1: define LOO weights and LOO decomposition.

By reproducing from (
88
),

I_{2}^{(U)}(x_{0};\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)\,\Delta w(\tilde{X}_{u})-\mathbb{E}[u(X;\theta)\Delta w(X)].

(89)

Conditional on covariates,
u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)
is deterministic; hence one cannot apply conditional Hoeffding
by treating
u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)-\mathbb{E}[u(X;\theta)]
as independent noise.
For each
u\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, define

\hat{T}_{K}^{(-u)}:=\hat{T}_{K}-\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\,K_{\tilde{X}_{u}}\otimes K_{\tilde{X}_{u}},\quad\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}^{(-u)}:=(\hat{T}_{K}^{(-u)}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}},\quad\Delta w^{(-u)}:=\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}^{(-u)}-w_{x_{0},\lambda}.

Using fixed scaling
1/N^{\prime}
makes the rank-one update exact. Add and subtract the LOO quantities in (
89
):

\displaystyle I_{2}^{(U)}(x_{0};\theta)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\Big(u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)\,\Delta w^{(-u)}(\tilde{X}_{u})-\mathbb{E}[u(X;\theta)\Delta w^{(-u)}(X)|\bar{X}^{(-u)}]\Big)}_{=:\penalty\ \tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}(x_{0};\theta)}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)\big(\Delta w-\Delta w^{(-u)}\big)(\tilde{X}_{u})}_{=:\penalty\ \mathrm{GAP}_{U,1}(x_{0};\theta)}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\Big(\mathbb{E}[u(X;\theta)\Delta w^{(-u)}(X)|\bar{X}^{(-u)}]-\mathbb{E}[u(X;\theta)\Delta w(X)]\Big)}_{=:\penalty\ \mathrm{GAP}_{U,2}(x_{0};\theta)}.

Thus

|I_{2}^{(U)}|\leq|\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}|+\mathrm{GAP}_{U},\qquad\mathrm{GAP}_{U}:=|\mathrm{GAP}_{U,1}|+|\mathrm{GAP}_{U,2}|.

Step A.2: GAP bound.

By Lemma
6
, on
\mathscr{E}
,
using
|u(\cdot;\theta)|\leq B_{f}(\theta)
and (
34
),

\displaystyle|\mathrm{GAP}_{U,1}|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)\big(\Delta w-\Delta w^{(-u)}\big)(\tilde{X}_{u})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}|u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)|\ \sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{1\leq j\leq N^{\prime}}|\Delta w(x)-\Delta w^{(-j)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq B_{f}(\theta)\cdot c\,\kappa^{2}B_{\phi}^{2}\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

For
\mathrm{GAP}_{U,2}
, by Jensen and the same uniform gap bound,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{GAP}_{U,2}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\Big|\mathbb{E}\big[u(X;\theta)\big(\Delta w^{(-u)}(X)-\Delta w(X)\big)|\bar{X}^{(-u)}\big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\big[|u(X;\theta)|\cdot\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|\Delta w^{(-u)}(x)-\Delta w(x)||\bar{X}^{(-u)}\big]

\displaystyle\leq B_{f}(\theta)\cdot c\,\kappa^{2}B_{\phi}^{2}\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

Summing the two bounds proves

\mathrm{GAP}_{U}(x_{0};\theta)\leq c\,B_{f}(\theta)\,\kappa^{2}\,B_{\phi}^{2}\,\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

(90)

Step A.3: main term
\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}
.

Write

\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}(x_{0};\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}Z_{u},\qquad Z_{u}:=u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)\Delta w^{(-u)}(\tilde{X}_{u})-\mathbb{E}[u(X;\theta)\Delta w^{(-u)}(X)|\bar{X}^{(-u)}].

For each fixed
u
,

\mathbb{E}[Z_{u}|\bar{X}^{(-u)}]=0,

since
\Delta w^{(-u)}
is measurable w.r.t.
\bar{X}^{(-u)}
and
\tilde{X}_{u}\perp\perp\bar{X}^{(-u)}
.
However, the collection
\{Z_{u}\}
is not conditionally independent across
u
and their LOO functions share most training points,
so we use the exponential Efron–Stein method.

Let
\tilde{X}_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\tilde{X}_{N}^{\prime}
be an independent copy.
For each
v
, define

\tilde{X}^{(v)}:=(\tilde{X}_{1},\dots,\tilde{X}_{v-1},\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime},\tilde{X}_{v+1},\dots,\tilde{X}_{N}),

and let
\tilde{I}_{2,(v)}^{(U)}(x_{0};\theta)
denote the statistic computed from
\tilde{X}^{(v)}

with all LOO quantities recomputed accordingly.
Define the Efron–Stein variance proxy

V:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}-\tilde{I}_{2,(v)}^{(U)}\big)^{2}\ \Big|\ \bar{X}\Big].

A standard exponential Efron–Stein inequality
(
Boucheron
et al. 2003
, Theorem 6.9)
implies: for all
\delta\in(0,1)
,
with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\big|\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}-\mathbb{E}[\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}]\big|\leq\sqrt{2V\log(2/\delta)}+\frac{b\log(2/\delta)}{3},

(91)

whenever the replace-one increments are bounded by
b
almost surely.
Here
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}]=0
by the tower property.

Fix
v
. Write

\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}-\tilde{I}_{2,(v)}^{(U)}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}(Z_{u}-Z_{u,(v)}).

For the own term
u=v
,
\Delta w^{(-v)}
is computed with
\tilde{X}_{v}
removed, hence replacing
\tilde{X}_{v}

does not change the training set for
\Delta w^{(-v)}
and

Z_{v}-Z_{v,(v)}=u(\tilde{X}_{v};\theta)\Delta w^{(-v)}(\tilde{X}_{v})-u(\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime};\theta)\Delta w^{(-v)}(\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}).

(92)

Conditioning on
\bar{X}^{(-v)}
and using
|u|\leq B_{f}(\theta)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\big[(Z_{v}-Z_{v,(v)})^{2}|\bar{X}^{(-v)}\big]&\leq 4B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,\mathbb{E}\big[(\Delta w^{(-v)}(X))^{2}|\bar{X}^{(-v)}\big]\\
&\leq 4B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,\|\Delta w^{(-v)}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}.\end{split}

(93)

For cross terms
u\neq v
,
\tilde{X}_{u}
is unchanged but
\Delta w^{(-u)}
changes because its pooled design
contains
\tilde{X}_{v}
vs.
\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}
. Using
|u|\leq B_{f}(\theta)
and triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\begin{split}|Z_{u}-Z_{u,(v)}|&\leq B_{f}(\theta)\big|\Delta w^{(-u)}(\tilde{X}_{u})-\Delta w^{(-u)}_{(v)}(\tilde{X}_{u})\big|\\
&\quad+B_{f}(\theta)\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\Delta w^{(-u)}(X)-\Delta w^{(-u)}_{(v)}(X)\big|\ \Big|\ \bar{X},\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}\Big],\end{split}

(94)

where
\Delta w^{(-u)}_{(v)}
is the LOO function computed after replacing
\tilde{X}_{v}
by
\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}
.

On
\mathscr{E}
, the same arguments as Lemma
6

yield the replace-one stability bound

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\big|\Delta w^{(-u)}(x)-\Delta w^{(-u)}_{(v)}(x)\big|\leq c\,\kappa^{2}\,B_{\phi}^{2}\,\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}.

(95)

Plugging (
95
) into (
94
) gives, on
\mathscr{E}
,

|Z_{u}-Z_{u,(v)}|\leq c\,B_{f}(\theta)\,\kappa^{2}\,B_{\phi}^{2}\,\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}},\qquad(u\neq v).

(96)

Using
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}-\tilde{I}_{2,(v)}^{(U)}\big)^{2}\ \Big|\ \bar{X}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[(Z_{v}-Z_{v,(v)})^{2}|\bar{X}\big]+\frac{2}{N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{u\neq v}(Z_{u}-Z_{u,(v)})\Big)^{2}\ \Big|\ \bar{X}\Big].

(97)

For the own term, by (
93
),

\mathbb{E}[(Z_{v}-Z_{v,(v)})^{2}|\bar{X}]\leq c\,B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,\|\Delta w^{(-v)}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}.

(98)

For the cross term, let
a_{u}:=Z_{u}-Z_{u,(v)}
for
u\neq v
.
Expanding the square gives

\Big(\sum_{u\neq v}a_{u}\Big)^{2}=\sum_{u\neq v}a_{u}^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\neq u^{\prime}\\
u,u^{\prime}\neq v\end{subarray}}a_{u}a_{u^{\prime}}.

We now argue that the cross-products vanish after a hierarchical
conditioning step that freezes the common coupling variable
\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}
but
leaves the evaluation coordinates
\{\tilde{X}_{u}:u\neq v\}
random inside the
inner expectation.
More precisely, by the tower property,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{u\neq v}a_{u}\Big)^{2}\ \Big|\ \bar{X}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{u\neq v}a_{u}\Big)^{2}\ \Big|\ \bar{X},\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}\Big]\ \Big|\ \bar{X}\Big].

(99)

Conditional on
(\bar{X},\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime})
, the statistic
Z_{u,(v)}
is constructed
with the same centering mechanism as
Z_{u}
, so that for each
u\neq v
,

\mathbb{E}[a_{u}|\bar{X},\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}]=0,\qquad(u\neq v).

Moreover, given
(\bar{X},\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime})
, the randomness across different
evaluation coordinates
\{\tilde{X}_{u}\}_{u\neq v}
enters
a_{u}
only through the
centered evaluation at index
u
, which yields the conditional orthogonality

\mathbb{E}[a_{u}a_{u^{\prime}}|\bar{X},\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}]=0,\qquad(u\neq u^{\prime},\ u,u^{\prime}\neq v).

(100)

Plugging (
100
) into the expansion of the square gives

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{u\neq v}a_{u}\Big)^{2}\ \Big|\ \bar{X},\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}\Big]=\sum_{u\neq v}\mathbb{E}[a_{u}^{2}|\bar{X},\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime}].

Taking
\mathbb{E}[\cdot|\bar{X}]
on both sides and using (
99
)
yields the variance additivity:

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{u\neq v}a_{u}\Big)^{2}\ \Big|\ \bar{X}\Big]=\sum_{u\neq v}\mathbb{E}[a_{u}^{2}|\bar{X}].

Finally, applying (
96
) gives, on
\mathscr{E}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{u\neq v}(Z_{u}-Z_{u,(v)})\Big)^{2}|\bar{X}\Big]&=\sum_{u\neq v}\mathbb{E}[a_{u}^{2}|\bar{X}]\\
&\leq\sum_{u\neq v}\Big(c\,B_{f}(\theta)\,\kappa^{2}\,B_{\phi}^{2}\,\frac{D(\lambda)}{N^{\prime}}\Big)^{2}\\
&\leq c\,B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,\kappa^{4}\,B_{\phi}^{4}\,D(\lambda)^{2}\cdot\frac{N}{N^{\prime 2}}.\end{split}

(101)

Plugging (
98
) and (
101
) into (
97
)
and summing over
v
gives

V\leq c\,\frac{B_{f}^{2}(\theta)}{N^{2}}\sum_{v=1}^{N}\|\Delta w^{(-v)}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}^{2}\ +\ c\,B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,\kappa^{4}\,B_{\phi}^{4}\,D(\lambda)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{\prime 2}}.

(102)

Lemma
5
applies verbatim to each LOO operator
\hat{T}_{K}^{(-v)}
.
Thus, with probability at least
1-(2N^{\prime})^{-1}
, uniformly over
v\leq N
,

\max_{1\leq v\leq N}\|\Delta w^{(-v)}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{X})}\leq c\,B_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}}.

(103)

Plugging (
103
) into (
102
) yields on
\mathscr{E}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}V&\leq c\,\frac{B_{f}^{2}(\theta)}{N^{2}}\cdot N\cdot B_{\phi}^{2}\frac{D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}+c\,B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,\kappa^{4}\,B_{\phi}^{4}\,D(\lambda)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{\prime 2}}\\
&=c\,B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,B_{\phi}^{2}\,D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)\cdot\frac{\log N^{\prime}}{NN^{\prime}}+c\,B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,\kappa^{4}\,B_{\phi}^{4}\,D(\lambda)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{\prime 2}}\\
&\lesssim B_{f}^{2}(\theta)\,B_{\phi}^{2}\,D(\lambda)^{2}\cdot\frac{\log N^{\prime}}{NN^{\prime}},\end{split}

(104)

where the second term is comparable and can be absorbed since
N^{\prime}\geq N
.

Fix
v
. Using the decomposition

\tilde{I}_{2}^{(U)}-\tilde{I}_{2,(v)}^{(U)}=\frac{1}{N}(Z_{v}-Z_{v,(v)})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u\neq v}(Z_{u}-Z_{u,(v)}),

we bound the two parts separately.
For the own term, by (
92
) and
|u|\leq B_{f}(\theta)
,

|Z_{v}-Z_{v,(v)}|\leq B_{f}(\theta)\big(|\Delta w^{(-v)}(\tilde{X}_{v})|+|\Delta w^{(-v)}(\tilde{X}_{v}^{\prime})|\big)\leq 2B_{f}(\theta)\sup_{x}|\Delta w^{(-v)}(x)|.

By bounded evaluation in RKHS,
|h(x)|\leq\kappa\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}
, so
\sup_{x}|\Delta w^{(-v)}(x)|\leq\kappa\|\Delta w^{(-v)}\|_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Moreover, on
\mathscr{E}
one has the operator inequality
\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}\preceq 2T_{\lambda}^{-1}

as in Lemma
5
, and therefore the same resolvent calculation controlling

\|\Delta w\|_{\mathcal{H}}
yields
