\displaystyle:=\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta\overline{w}(X_{i})\partial_{\theta}r(Y_{i},X_{i};\theta)\Big\}+\Big\{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{2}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{m}}\Delta w^{(3-m)}(\tilde{X}_{u})\partial_{\theta}u(\tilde{X}_{u};\theta)\Big\}-J_{3}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta).

Then for every
\theta
,

\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)-J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=J_{1}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta)+J_{2}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta)+J_{3}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta).

Step 2: uniform bound for
J_{1}^{\mathrm{split}}
.

By Assumption
2
, for all
\theta\in\Theta_{0}
,
|\partial_{\theta}r(Y,X;\theta)|\leq G_{Y-f}
and
|\partial_{\theta}u(X;\theta)|\leq G_{f}
.
Also
\mathbb{E}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)^{2}]\leq D(x_{0};\lambda)
and
\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\infty}\leq B_{\phi}\sqrt{D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)}
.
Applying the same scalar Bernstein argument as in Lemma
4
to the labeled and unlabeled terms and taking a union bound yields:
for all sufficiently large
N
, with probability at least
1-\frac{8}{N}
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|J_{1}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta)|\leq 2\sqrt{2\log N}\sqrt{D(x_{0};\lambda)}\left(\frac{G_{Y-f}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{G_{f}}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

(178)

Step 3: uniform bound for
J_{2}^{\mathrm{split}}
on
\mathscr{E}
.

On
\mathscr{E}
, the proof of Lemma
12
(ii) applies verbatim after replacing

r(Y,X;\theta)
and
u(X;\theta)
by
\partial_{\theta}r(Y,X;\theta)
and
\partial_{\theta}u(X;\theta)
,
and replacing
B_{Y-f}(\theta),B_{f}(\theta)
by
G_{Y-f},G_{f}
.
Let
c_{0}>0
be the absolute constant in Lemma
5
.
Thus on
\mathscr{E}
, with probability at least
1-\frac{6}{N}-\frac{2}{n^{2}}-\frac{4}{N^{2}}
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|J_{2}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta)|\leq B_{\phi}\sqrt{D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)}\left[(2c_{0}+4)G_{Y-f}\frac{\log n}{n}+\Big(c_{0}G_{Y-f}+(4c_{0}+8)G_{f}\Big)\frac{\log N}{N}\right].

(179)

Step 4: uniform bound for
J_{3}^{\mathrm{split}}
on
\mathscr{E}
.

By definition of
\Delta\overline{w}
,

J_{3}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta)=\langle\Delta\overline{w},\mu^{1}(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=1}^{2}\langle\Delta w^{(m)},\mu^{1}(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

where
\mu^{1}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}[\partial_{\theta}\ell(Y;\theta)K_{X}]
as in (
129
).
For each fixed
m
, on
\mathscr{E}_{m}
the
J_{3}
argument in Step 4 of Lemma
9
applies to the fold operator

\hat{T}_{K}^{(m)}
with sample size
|\mathcal{I}_{m}|=N/2
, and uses only Assumptions
2
and
4
.
In particular, for all sufficiently large
N
, on
\mathscr{E}_{m}
and with probability at least
1-\frac{6}{N}
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\big|\langle\Delta w^{(m)},\mu^{1}(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big|\leq 64B_{\phi}(G_{Y-f}+G_{f})\sqrt{\frac{D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N}{N}}.

By a union bound over
m=1,2
, on
\mathscr{E}
and with probability at least
1-\frac{12}{N}
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|J_{3}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta)|\leq 64B_{\phi}(G_{Y-f}+G_{f})\sqrt{\frac{D(\lambda)D(x_{0};\lambda)\log N}{N}}.

(180)

Step 5: conclude.

Intersect the events in (
178
), (
179
), and (
180
).
On
\mathscr{E}
, this intersection has probability at least

1-\frac{8}{N}-\left(\frac{6}{N}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}+\frac{4}{N^{2}}\right)-\frac{12}{N}=1-\frac{26}{N}-\frac{2}{n^{2}}-\frac{4}{N^{2}}.

On this event,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)-J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)\big|\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|J_{1}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta)|+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|J_{2}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta)|+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}|J_{3}^{\mathrm{split}}(x_{0};\theta)|.

Under (
155
), each upper bound in (
178
), (
179
), and (
180
)
tends to
0
as
N\to\infty
(using also
n\wedge N\to\infty
).
Hence there exists
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)-J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)\big|\leq c_{J}/2\qquad\text{on }\mathscr{E}.

Therefore, using Assumption
3
, for all
\theta\in\Theta_{0}
,

|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)|\geq|J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)|-\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)-J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)\big|\geq c_{J}-c_{J}/2=c_{J}/2,

which proves (
156
). The inverse bound follows immediately:
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\big|\hat{J}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)^{-1}\big|\leq\frac{2}{c_{J}}
.
∎

E.3
Technical Novelties and Comparison with Classical KRR Theory

While our theoretical framework builds upon the mathematical tools of RKHS, the conditional estimation nature of PPCI introduces fundamental challenges that classical Kernel Ridge Regression (KRR) theory cannot resolve. Below, we highlight three major technical departures from standard KRR analyses, which also elucidate the necessity of our proof techniques.

1. Shared-Design Dependence and the
I_{2}
Interaction.

In classical non-parametric KRR, the objective is to estimate a global regression function
f_{0}(x)
from responses
Y_{i}=f_{0}(X_{i})+\epsilon_{i}
. By the Representer Theorem, the empirical KRR estimator evaluated at a test point
x_{0}
admits a closed-form linear representation:

\hat{f}(x_{0})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})Y_{i},

where the empirical weight function is explicitly defined as
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}=(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}}
. Substituting the response yields the exact error decomposition
\hat{f}(x_{0})-f_{0}(x_{0})=\text{Bias}(x_{0})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})\epsilon_{i}
. A standard conditioning argument perfectly decouples this variance term: conditionally on the covariates
\bar{X}
(where
\bar{X}
denotes the design matrix), the empirical weights
\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})
become deterministic constants. Since the observation noise satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\epsilon_{i}|\bar{X}]=0
, the cross-term strictly vanishes in expectation, yielding a straightforward variance bound governed by
\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i}\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}(X_{i})^{2}\sigma^{2}
.

In sharp contrast, our non-split PPCI procedure couples the empirical weights and the evaluation scores on the same unlabeled dataset. The interaction term governing this coupling,
I_{2}^{(U)}
, includes
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{u=1}^{N}\Delta w(\tilde{X}_{u})\{\ell(f(\tilde{X}_{u});\theta)-\mathbb{E}[\ell(f(X);\theta)]\}
, where
\Delta w=\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}-w_{x_{0},\lambda}
. Here, the covariate
\tilde{X}_{u}
acts simultaneously to construct the weight
\Delta w
and to generate the score fluctuation. Conditioning on
\bar{X}
no longer yields a mean-zero noise, meaning
\mathbb{E}[\ell(f(\tilde{X}_{u});\theta)-\mathbb{E}[\ell(f(X);\theta)]|\bar{X}]\neq 0
. This shared-design dependence completely invalidates standard empirical process arguments and martingale techniques.
While our two-fold splitting version (Algorithm
1
) intentionally circumvents this by decoupling the data to provide a simple baseline, establishing the full-sample theory requires much heavier machinery. To untangle this dependence without sample-splitting, we develop a Leave-One-Out stability analysis leveraging exact noiseless KRR representations (Lemma
6
) to rigorously prove that this highly entangled
I_{2}
term is asymptotically negligible.

2. Avoiding the RKHS Penalty for the
I_{3}
term.

Another critical departure arises in bounding the operator approximation error,
I_{3}=\langle\hat{w}_{x_{0},\lambda}-w_{x_{0},\lambda},\mu(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. In classical KRR, the empirical estimator
\hat{f}
targets the population regularized function
f_{\lambda}=(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}T_{K}f_{0}
. By the resolvent identity, the pointwise estimation error involves bounding the term
\langle(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}(T_{K}-\hat{T}_{K})f_{\lambda},K_{x_{0}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. Classical proofs routinely rely on the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in
\mathcal{H}
to decouple the operator approximation error from the target function, yielding the bound:

\big|\langle(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}(T_{K}-\hat{T}_{K})f_{\lambda},K_{x_{0}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big|\leq\|(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}(T_{K}-\hat{T}_{K})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\|f_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\|K_{x_{0}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

This strategy is sharp in classical settings because the global target
f_{0}\in\mathcal{H}
is a fixed underlying truth; consequently, its regularized version satisfies
\|f_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|f_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}=O(1)
.

However, in our framework, the target object is the localized weight function itself:
w_{x_{0},\lambda}=(T_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}K_{x_{0}}
. By applying the same resolvent identity, the error term
I_{3}
expands as
\langle(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}(T_{K}-\hat{T}_{K})w_{x_{0},\lambda},\mu(\theta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. Directly applying the classical Cauchy-Schwarz argument here would yield:

|I_{3}|\leq\|(\hat{T}_{K}+\lambda I)^{-1}(T_{K}-\hat{T}_{K})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\|\mu(\theta)\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

As
\lambda\to 0
,
w_{x_{0},\lambda}
approximates a Dirac delta at
x_{0}
. Since the delta function does not reside in the RKHS, the RKHS norm of our target explodes at the rate of
\|w_{x_{0},\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\approx D(x_{0};\lambda)/\lambda\propto\lambda^{-1-d/(2m)}
. Isolating this exploding norm via Cauchy-Schwarz inevitably incurs an additional
1/\sqrt{\lambda}
penalty, resulting in a loose and suboptimal convergence rate.

To achieve a tight bound, we completely bypass the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in
\mathcal{H}
. Instead, we project the RKHS inner product back into a scalar empirical process (Step 3 in the proof of Lemma
8
):
T_{1}=\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}\{w_{x_{0},\lambda}(\bar{X}_{i})b(\bar{X}_{i})-\mathbb{E}[a(X)b(X)]\}
. This allows us to bound the variance proxy using the
L^{2}(\rho_{X})
norm rather than the
\mathcal{H}
norm. By noting that
\mathbb{E}[w_{x_{0},\lambda}(X)^{2}]\leq D(x_{0};\lambda)\propto\lambda^{-d/(2m)}
, we successfully eliminate the extra variance explosion, recovering the minimax-optimal rate. We remark that this strategy of reducing abstract RKHS operations to scalar empirical processes is a recurring technique throughout our analysis, which proves instrumental in establishing tight rates for other error components as well.

3. Uniform Control of the Out-of-RKHS Jacobian.

Finally, classical KRR analyses typically conclude once the regression function itself is bounded. However, in PPCI, statistical inference fundamentally relies on solving the empirical equation
\hat{\eta}_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=0
. The asymptotic behavior of the estimator is therefore governed by the localized Jacobian matrix,
J_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)=\partial_{\theta}\eta_{\lambda}(x_{0};\theta)
.

A major technical hurdle is that even if the conditional moment
\eta(\cdot;\theta)
belongs to
\mathcal{H}
, the derivative of the loss,
\partial_{\theta}\ell
, frequently falls outside the RKHS
\mathcal{H}
. Standard KRR convergence theories cannot cover this out-of-RKHS object. To guarantee the asymptotic validity of our confidence intervals, we must independently establish uniform stability bounds over
\Theta_{0}
for this empirical Jacobian. We show that provided the regularization parameter
\lambda
decays sufficiently slowly, the out-of-RKHS empirical Jacobian does not degenerate, a property entirely absent from the global KRR literature.
